# Daily Summary: 2025-03-06

### Large Language Models for Multilingual Previously Fact-Checked Claim Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.02737v1)
- **Authors**: Ivan Vykopal, Matúš Pikuliak, Simon Ostermann, Tatiana Anikina, Michal Gregor, Marián Šimko
- **Abstract**: In our era of widespread false information, human fact-checkers often face the challenge of duplicating efforts when verifying claims that may have already been addressed in other countries or languages. As false information transcends linguistic boundaries, the ability to automatically detect previously fact-checked claims across languages has become an increasingly important task. This paper presents the first comprehensive evaluation of large language models (LLMs) for multilingual previously fact-checked claim detection. We assess seven LLMs across 20 languages in both monolingual and cross-lingual settings. Our results show that while LLMs perform well for high-resource languages, they struggle with low-resource languages. Moreover, translating original texts into English proved to be beneficial for low-resource languages. These findings highlight the potential of LLMs for multilingual previously fact-checked claim detection and provide a foundation for further research on this promising application of LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) for multilingual previously fact-checked claim detection (PFCD). The authors evaluate seven LLMs across 20 languages, assessing their performance in both monolingual and cross-lingual settings using various prompting techniques.  A new annotated dataset, AMC-16K, comprising 16,000 pairs of social media posts and fact-checked claims, is introduced to validate the pipeline. The key findings indicate that while LLMs perform well with high-resource languages, they struggle with low-resource languages. Translation of texts into English improves performance for low-resource languages.  The effectiveness of prompting techniques varies depending on the LLM size and training.  Mistral Large and C4AI Command R+ models perform well overall.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents the *first* comprehensive evaluation of LLMs for multilingual PFCD across a substantial number of languages (20). While previous work has touched on PFCD in multilingual settings using embedding-based models, the systematic exploration of LLMs with a controlled set of prompting strategies and the introduction of the AMC-16K dataset constitutes a significant contribution. The study goes beyond a simple performance evaluation, offering insights into the impact of translation, prompting techniques, and language resources.

*   **Significance:** The task of automatically detecting previously fact-checked claims is crucial for combating the spread of misinformation, especially across linguistic boundaries. By demonstrating the potential of LLMs (and their limitations) in this area, the paper contributes valuable knowledge for the development of automated fact-checking tools. The findings related to the benefits of translating low-resource language inputs to English and the effectiveness of different prompting methods offer practical guidance for researchers and practitioners. The open-sourcing of the code and the release of the new annotated dataset further enhance the impact.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The study is well-designed and comprehensive, covering a wide range of LLMs, languages, and prompting strategies.
    *   **New Dataset:** The creation and release of the AMC-16K dataset fill a critical gap in the available resources for multilingual PFCD research.
    *   **Actionable Insights:** The paper provides practical insights regarding the selection of appropriate LLMs and techniques for different language settings.
    *   **Error Analysis:**  While concise, the error analysis section provides some initial insights into the types of reasoning errors LLMs are prone to, hinting at future research directions.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Error Analysis:** While an error analysis is presented, the insights remain somewhat superficial. A more detailed investigation into specific failure modes (e.g., how LLMs handle subtle semantic differences across languages, the impact of different writing styles, or the specific types of claims that are more difficult to detect) would enhance the paper's contribution.
    *   **Dataset Bias:** The use of an embedding model for the data selection step introduces a potential bias in the annotation data (as the data are relevant with regards to the retriever). Also, while attempts are made to address the lack of complete annotation in the MultiClaim dataset, the reliance on retrieved examples still limits the extent of potential recall measurements.
    *   **Model Selection:** The exclusion of closed-source models (e.g., GPT-4) is a stated limitation. While understandable, comparing against a state-of-the-art closed-source model could have provided a more definitive benchmark and highlighted the remaining performance gap.
    *   **Limited exploration of generative models:** While the study presents a great overview over several models, only discriminative models classifying post-claim pairs are compared. Generative models could solve claim matching in a more fine grained manner, which should be adressed in further research.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper is likely to have a substantial influence on the field. It will serve as a valuable reference point for future research on multilingual PFCD, particularly for studies involving LLMs. The AMC-16K dataset will likely become a standard benchmark for evaluating new approaches. The findings regarding the use of translation and the selection of appropriate prompting techniques will guide the development of more effective and practical PFCD systems.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by providing a comprehensive evaluation of LLMs for multilingual PFCD. The creation and release of the new annotated dataset enhance the paper's impact. While the paper does have its limitations, the strengths and potential influence of the paper justify a score of 7. The detailed findings and practical guidelines are expected to stimulate further research and development in this vital area. A higher score could be warranted with a more detailed error analysis and a broader consideration of LLM architectures (including closed-source models and other approaches).

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### From Metaphor to Mechanism: How LLMs Decode Traditional Chinese Medicine Symbolic Language for Modern Clinical Relevance
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.02760v1)
- **Authors**: Jiacheng Tang, Nankai Wu, Fan Gao, Chengxiao Dai, Mengyao Zhao, Xinjie Zhao
- **Abstract**: Metaphorical expressions are abundant in Traditional Chinese Medicine (TCM), conveying complex disease mechanisms and holistic health concepts through culturally rich and often abstract terminology. Bridging these metaphors to anatomically driven Western medical (WM) concepts poses significant challenges for both automated language processing and real-world clinical practice. To address this gap, we propose a novel multi-agent and chain-of-thought (CoT) framework designed to interpret TCM metaphors accurately and map them to WM pathophysiology. Specifically, our approach combines domain-specialized agents (TCM Expert, WM Expert) with a Coordinator Agent, leveraging stepwise chain-of-thought prompts to ensure transparent reasoning and conflict resolution. We detail a methodology for building a metaphor-rich TCM dataset, discuss strategies for effectively integrating multi-agent collaboration and CoT reasoning, and articulate the theoretical underpinnings that guide metaphor interpretation across distinct medical paradigms. We present a comprehensive system design and highlight both the potential benefits and limitations of our approach, while leaving placeholders for future experimental validation. Our work aims to support clinical decision-making, cross-system educational initiatives, and integrated healthcare research, ultimately offering a robust scaffold for reconciling TCM's symbolic language with the mechanistic focus of Western medicine.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper, "From Metaphor to Mechanism: How LLMs Decode Traditional Chinese Medicine Symbolic Language for Modern Clinical Relevance."

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of bridging the gap between the metaphorical language of Traditional Chinese Medicine (TCM) and the mechanistic understanding of Western medicine (WM). It proposes a novel multi-agent, chain-of-thought (CoT) framework utilizing Large Language Models (LLMs) to interpret TCM metaphors and map them to WM pathophysiology. The framework consists of a TCM Expert agent, a WM Expert agent, and a Coordinator agent, all leveraging CoT prompting to ensure transparent reasoning and conflict resolution. The paper describes a methodology for building a metaphor-rich TCM dataset and discusses strategies for integrating multi-agent collaboration and CoT reasoning.  While the paper outlines the system design and its potential benefits, it acknowledges the need for future experimental validation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The approach of using a multi-agent system with CoT prompting to bridge TCM and WM is relatively novel. While LLMs have been used in healthcare applications, specifically targeting the integration of TCM knowledge with LLMs is less explored. The explicit formalization of TCM concepts using fuzzy logic, symbolic logic, and Bayesian networks is also a valuable contribution. The paper presents a structured approach to a complex problem where cultural understanding is key, and thus tackles the difficulty of metaphor interpretation from a perspective that current LLMs struggle with by themselves.

**Significance:** The significance lies in the potential to improve cross-disciplinary communication, clinical decision-making, and educational initiatives in healthcare. If successfully implemented, the framework could facilitate better understanding and integration of TCM principles into modern medical practice. This has implications for personalized medicine, integrative healthcare research, and broader acceptance of TCM within the global medical community. It tackles a key hurdle hindering TCM's acceptance by formalizing the somewhat obtuse reasoning present within the field.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-defined framework:** The paper clearly articulates the architecture of the multi-agent system, the role of each agent, and the integration of CoT reasoning.
*   **Formalization of TCM concepts:** The use of symbolic logic, fuzzy sets, and Bayesian networks provides a rigorous foundation for interpreting TCM metaphors.
*   **Addresses a critical challenge:** It tackles a significant hurdle in integrating TCM with Western medicine: the lack of a common language and understanding.
*   **Comprehensive Dataset Construction:** The authors took the time to construct a comprehensive dataset from both WM and TCM sources. This dataset will provide a large benefit to the project overall.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Lack of Experimental Validation:** The most significant weakness is the absence of empirical results. The paper describes the system design and dataset creation but does not provide concrete evidence of its effectiveness. The discussion of results feels hypothetical without the presence of any experiments.
*   **Limited discussion on dataset availability:** While the authors describe the dataset construction methodology, the paper does not indicate whether the dataset will be made publicly available.
*   **Scalability concerns:** While the framework addresses the core challenge, it might be computationally expensive to implement and scale to handle large volumes of clinical data, especially with the use of multiple specialized agents.
*   **Potential for oversimplification:** Mapping TCM metaphors to WM pathophysiology inevitably involves some degree of simplification, which could lead to a loss of nuanced meaning.
*   **The WM validation suffers from "garbage in, garbage out"**: The reliance on WM experts to evaluate results could fall prey to bias depending on the expertise of the experts themselves, as WM is also not perfect.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the field by:

*   Inspiring further research on using multi-agent systems and CoT reasoning for cross-cultural knowledge translation in healthcare.
*   Providing a framework for building more sophisticated LLMs that can interpret and integrate TCM principles.
*   Facilitating the development of clinical decision support tools that incorporate both TCM and WM perspectives.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper presents a well-structured and potentially significant approach, the lack of experimental validation is a major drawback. The absence of concrete results makes it difficult to assess the true effectiveness of the framework. The lack of clear evaluation makes it hard to score higher. The approach is novel and relevant, thus it justifies a score above the midpoint.

**Score: 6/10**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### InSerter: Speech Instruction Following with Unsupervised Interleaved Pre-training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.02769v1)
- **Authors**: Dingdong Wang, Jin Xu, Ruihang Chu, Zhifang Guo, Xiong Wang, Jincenzi Wu, Dongchao Yang, Shengpeng Ji, Junyang Lin
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in speech large language models (SpeechLLMs) have attracted considerable attention. Nonetheless, current methods exhibit suboptimal performance in adhering to speech instructions. Notably, the intelligence of models significantly diminishes when processing speech-form input as compared to direct text-form input. Prior work has attempted to mitigate this semantic inconsistency between speech and text representations through techniques such as representation and behavior alignment, which involve the meticulous design of data pairs during the post-training phase. In this paper, we introduce a simple and scalable training method called InSerter, which stands for Interleaved Speech-Text Representation Pre-training. InSerter is designed to pre-train large-scale unsupervised speech-text sequences, where the speech is synthesized from randomly selected segments of an extensive text corpus using text-to-speech conversion. Consequently, the model acquires the ability to generate textual continuations corresponding to the provided speech segments, obviating the need for intensive data design endeavors. To systematically evaluate speech instruction-following capabilities, we introduce SpeechInstructBench, the first comprehensive benchmark specifically designed for speech-oriented instruction-following tasks. Our proposed InSerter achieves SOTA performance in SpeechInstructBench and demonstrates superior or competitive results across diverse speech processing tasks.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "InSerter: Speech Instruction Following with Unsupervised Interleaved Pre-training":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces InSerter, a novel pre-training method designed to improve speech instruction following capabilities in Speech Large Language Models (SpeechLLMs). InSerter leverages large-scale unsupervised speech-text sequences where speech segments are synthesized from random text segments using text-to-speech (TTS) conversion. This interleaved speech-text data is used to pre-train the model, enabling it to generate text continuations corresponding to provided speech segments. The method aims to address the performance gap between text-based and speech-based instructions in SpeechLLMs. To evaluate the method, the authors introduce SpeechInstructBench, a new comprehensive benchmark designed specifically for speech instruction-following tasks. The results show that InSerter achieves state-of-the-art performance on SpeechInstructBench and demonstrates superior or competitive results across other speech processing tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of InSerter – using unsupervised interleaved speech-text pre-training – has a degree of novelty. While other works have explored representation alignment or behavior alignment through paired speech-text data, the InSerter approach leverages readily available text data by converting it into speech, effectively creating synthetic paired data without extensive manual annotation. It simplifies the data creation process significantly. Furthermore, interleaving speech and text during pre-training is a sensible approach towards the alignment.

*   **Significance:** Addressing the performance gap between text-based and speech-based instructions in SpeechLLMs is a significant problem. Many downstream tasks would benefit from a more seamless interaction of speech and text. The introduction of SpeechInstructBench is also a significant contribution, providing a much-needed standardized and comprehensive benchmark for evaluating speech instruction following. Achieving SOTA performance on this benchmark, as well as demonstrating positive results in other speech tasks, strengthens the claim that InSerter has a beneficial impact.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Simplicity and Scalability:** The method is conceptually simple and highly scalable due to its reliance on readily available text data and TTS conversion.
    *   **Data Efficiency:** InSerter uses unsupervised data, reducing the need for costly and meticulously designed paired speech-text datasets.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** Demonstrates SOTA performance on SpeechInstructBench and competitive or superior performance on other speech processing tasks.
    *   **Novel SpeechInstructBench benchmark.**
    *   Well structured experiments.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on TTS Quality:** The performance of InSerter depends on the quality of the TTS model used to generate the speech segments. Imperfections or artifacts in the synthesized speech could potentially hinder the model's ability to learn effective speech representations.
    *   **Limited Generalizability:** The paper primarily evaluates InSerter on English and Chinese datasets. Further research is needed to assess its generalizability to other languages and speech scenarios.
    *   **Limited Explanation/Analysis:** The paper does not provide deep insight into the kinds of features that can be learned with InSerter. How and why it improves the model, aside from data augmentation, could be explained in further details.
    *   **Potential Bias amplification**: Converting text to speech can propagate and amplify biases embedded in the text. This is because the characteristics of voices used in TTS, even with voice prompts, are often skewed relative to the general population.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in SpeechLLMs by providing a simple, scalable, and effective pre-training method.  SpeechInstructBench could become a widely adopted benchmark, driving progress in speech instruction following. The idea of leveraging large amounts of raw text data using TTS for creating speech data could be expanded in many areas in SpeechLLMs.

*   **Rigorous Rationale for Score:**
    The paper tackles a key challenge within SpeechLLMs: how to better understand and respond to spoken instructions, making models more intuitive. The simplicity of InSerter paired with scalability, coupled with quantitative experimental results, is compelling. The weaknesses are also there: dependence on TTS, lack of deeper analysis, etc. This paper improves previous approaches, but it is not a paradigm shift. The paper's value lies in its practicality, data efficiency, new SOTA results and the introduction of a good benchmark, so the score is:
*   **Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 7/10

### Implicit Bias in LLMs: A Survey
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.02776v1)
- **Authors**: Xinru Lin, Luyang Li
- **Abstract**: Due to the implement of guardrails by developers, Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated exceptional performance in explicit bias tests. However, bias in LLMs may occur not only explicitly, but also implicitly, much like humans who consciously strive for impartiality yet still harbor implicit bias. The unconscious and automatic nature of implicit bias makes it particularly challenging to study. This paper provides a comprehensive review of the existing literature on implicit bias in LLMs. We begin by introducing key concepts, theories and methods related to implicit bias in psychology, extending them from humans to LLMs. Drawing on the Implicit Association Test (IAT) and other psychological frameworks, we categorize detection methods into three primary approaches: word association, task-oriented text generation and decision-making. We divide our taxonomy of evaluation metrics for implicit bias into two categories: single-value-based metrics and comparison-value-based metrics. We classify datasets into two types: sentences with masked tokens and complete sentences, incorporating datasets from various domains to reflect the broad application of LLMs. Although research on mitigating implicit bias in LLMs is still limited, we summarize existing efforts and offer insights on future challenges. We aim for this work to serve as a clear guide for researchers and inspire innovative ideas to advance exploration in this task.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "Implicit Bias in LLMs: A Survey" as accurately as possible based on the information provided.

**Summary:**

This survey paper aims to provide a comprehensive overview of research on implicit bias in Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors argue that while explicit bias in LLMs has been addressed to some extent, implicit biases remain a significant challenge.  The paper draws parallels with implicit bias in human psychology, introduces key concepts and methods for detecting and mitigating implicit bias, and categorizes existing research based on three main detection approaches: word association, task-oriented text generation, and decision-making. It also classifies evaluation metrics and datasets. Finally, the paper summarizes limited existing mitigation efforts and discusses future research directions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    The paper's novelty lies in its attempt to provide a systematic framework for understanding and classifying the scattered research on implicit bias in LLMs.  While individual papers might have explored specific aspects of implicit bias, this survey attempts to consolidate them under a unified structure. It is useful that the paper connects the concepts from human psychology like Implicit Association Tests (IAT) and applies them to the study of LLMs. The taxonomy of detection methods is also a contribution, though it's debatable how definitive or exhaustive it is.
*   **Significance:**

    The topic is undoubtedly significant. As LLMs become more pervasive, it's critical to understand and address biases that may not be immediately apparent but could still have harmful consequences. By highlighting the shift from explicit to implicit bias, the paper points towards a crucial area needing further research. The survey also serves as a useful resource for researchers entering this field, providing a starting point and overview of existing techniques.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper is well-structured and clearly defines its scope.
    *   The categorization of detection methods, datasets, and evaluation metrics provides a useful framework.
    *   Connecting concepts from psychology helps bridge the gap between disciplines.
    *   The discussion of future research directions is valuable for guiding further investigation.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper acknowledges that research on mitigating implicit bias is limited, which also restricts the scope of its review in this area.
    *   While the taxonomy is helpful, some of the categories could be further refined and elaborated upon. For example, the distinction between single-value and comparison-value metrics, while present, may be more closely interlinked than distinct.
    *   The evaluation could be strengthened by a more in-depth critical analysis of the limitations of current detection methods and the challenges in accurately quantifying implicit bias.
    *   The practical impact of the work described in the survey could be more clearly emphasized through real-world examples.
*   **Potential Influence:**

    The paper can serve as a guide for researchers, helping them to navigate the existing literature and identify promising research directions. By raising awareness about the importance of implicit bias and providing a framework for studying it, this work can contribute to the development of more fair, accurate, and trustworthy LLMs.

    Justification for Score:
    The paper is beneficial as a first step in identifying the implicit biases in LLMs. But the paper does not provide more than just definition and identification of some detection methods, evaluation metrics, datasets, and some mitigation techniques to counter implicit bias.
    The review of techniques is not exhaustive and the novelty of it is not too high, due to it being a survey of a well-identified problem. It is an important but preliminary step.
Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### RAAD-LLM: Adaptive Anomaly Detection Using LLMs and RAG Integration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.02800v1)
- **Authors**: Alicia Russell-Gilbert, Sudip Mittal, Shahram Rahimi, Maria Seale, Joseph Jabour, Thomas Arnold, Joshua Church
- **Abstract**: Anomaly detection in complex industrial environments poses unique challenges, particularly in contexts characterized by data sparsity and evolving operational conditions. Predictive maintenance (PdM) in such settings demands methodologies that are adaptive, transferable, and capable of integrating domain-specific knowledge. In this paper, we present RAAD-LLM, a novel framework for adaptive anomaly detection, leveraging large language models (LLMs) integrated with Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). This approach addresses the aforementioned PdM challenges. By effectively utilizing domain-specific knowledge, RAAD-LLM enhances the detection of anomalies in time series data without requiring fine-tuning on specific datasets. The framework's adaptability mechanism enables it to adjust its understanding of normal operating conditions dynamically, thus increasing detection accuracy. We validate this methodology through a real-world application for a plastics manufacturing plant and the Skoltech Anomaly Benchmark (SKAB). Results show significant improvements over our previous model with an accuracy increase from 70.7 to 89.1 on the real-world dataset. By allowing for the enriching of input series data with semantics, RAAD-LLM incorporates multimodal capabilities that facilitate more collaborative decision-making between the model and plant operators. Overall, our findings support RAAD-LLM's ability to revolutionize anomaly detection methodologies in PdM, potentially leading to a paradigm shift in how anomaly detection is implemented across various industries.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RAAD-LLM, a novel framework for anomaly detection in industrial predictive maintenance (PdM). It integrates large language models (LLMs) with Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) to overcome challenges like data scarcity and evolving operational conditions. The framework leverages domain-specific knowledge to enhance anomaly detection in time series data without dataset-specific fine-tuning. It incorporates an adaptability mechanism for dynamically adjusting to normal operating conditions and supports multimodal input.  The paper showcases RAAD-LLM's effectiveness through a plastics manufacturing plant application and the SKAB benchmark, demonstrating improved accuracy compared to prior models. A variant, RAAD-LLMv2, which leverages LlamaIndex for automated domain knowledge retrieval, is also evaluated.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the specific combination of LLMs, RAG, and adaptability within the context of PdM anomaly detection. While LLMs and RAG have been applied to other time series tasks, their targeted integration within this domain, emphasizing multimodal input and zero-shot transferability, provides a unique contribution. The introduction of both RAAD-LLM and RAAD-LLMv2 allows for comparison between manually curated domain knowledge versus dynamic knowledge retrieval, highlighting trade-offs in performance and scalability.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to improve anomaly detection in complex industrial settings where data is sparse, and conditions change. The zero-shot transfer learning aspect addresses a key limitation of traditional PdM models. The multimodal capability and emphasis on incorporating domain expertise are valuable for bridging the gap between AI systems and human operators, promoting adoption. The experimental results, demonstrating improved accuracy over existing methods, suggest practical utility.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear articulation of the problem and limitations of existing approaches.
    *   Well-defined framework architecture.
    *   Empirical validation on a real-world manufacturing dataset and a public benchmark.
    *   Emphasis on interpretable outputs and improved collaboration with domain experts.
    *   Addresses the crucial need for adapting to changing conditions.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While results are promising, the real-world dataset is relatively small, and the experiments could benefit from more extensive testing across diverse industrial processes.
    *   RAAD-LLMv2 demonstrated lower performance, highlighting the need for refinement in dynamic knowledge retrieval.
    *   The reliance on manually restructured domain knowledge (in RAAD-LLM) can be a bottleneck, limiting scalability.
    *   Comparisons to more recent state-of-the-art anomaly detection methods (beyond the ones included in SKAB benchmark) could strengthen the evaluation.
    *   Discussion of computational costs and scalability is limited.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence PdM methodologies by demonstrating the power of LLMs in integrating domain knowledge and adapting to changing conditions. Its focus on explainability and user collaboration is crucial for practical adoption. The insights gained from comparing RAAD-LLM and RAAD-LLMv2 contribute to the broader understanding of using LLMs and RAG for time series analysis. While the work presents a specific implementation, the underlying principles and architecture are applicable to other industrial domains.

**Rationale for Score:**

The work represents a solid contribution by introducing a novel framework with practical utility and a focus on bridging the gap between AI and human expertise in PdM. It provides clear empirical evidence of improved performance compared to a previous state-of-the-art, a baseline, and performs well when compared to other algorithms on a public benchmark.  The weaknesses, such as the limited dataset size and the need for more work on dynamic knowledge retrieval, are acknowledged and represent opportunities for future research.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Feynman-Kac Correctors in Diffusion: Annealing, Guidance, and Product of Experts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.02819v1)
- **Authors**: Marta Skreta, Tara Akhound-Sadegh, Viktor Ohanesian, Roberto Bondesan, Alán Aspuru-Guzik, Arnaud Doucet, Rob Brekelmans, Alexander Tong, Kirill Neklyudov
- **Abstract**: While score-based generative models are the model of choice across diverse domains, there are limited tools available for controlling inference-time behavior in a principled manner, e.g. for composing multiple pretrained models. Existing classifier-free guidance methods use a simple heuristic to mix conditional and unconditional scores to approximately sample from conditional distributions. However, such methods do not approximate the intermediate distributions, necessitating additional 'corrector' steps. In this work, we provide an efficient and principled method for sampling from a sequence of annealed, geometric-averaged, or product distributions derived from pretrained score-based models. We derive a weighted simulation scheme which we call Feynman-Kac Correctors (FKCs) based on the celebrated Feynman-Kac formula by carefully accounting for terms in the appropriate partial differential equations (PDEs). To simulate these PDEs, we propose Sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) resampling algorithms that leverage inference-time scaling to improve sampling quality. We empirically demonstrate the utility of our methods by proposing amortized sampling via inference-time temperature annealing, improving multi-objective molecule generation using pretrained models, and improving classifier-free guidance for text-to-image generation. Our code is available at https://github.com/martaskrt/fkc-diffusion.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces Feynman-Kac Correctors (FKCs), a principled method for controlling inference-time behavior in score-based generative models. FKCs enable sampling from annealed, geometrically-averaged, or product distributions derived from pre-trained models. The method accounts for terms in partial differential equations (PDEs) via weighted simulation schemes. Sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) resampling algorithms are proposed to simulate the PDEs, leveraging inference-time scaling for improved sampling. The utility of the approach is demonstrated by amortized sampling through temperature annealing, multi-objective molecule generation with pre-trained models, and improved classifier-free guidance in text-to-image generation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using the Feynman-Kac formula for controlling inference in diffusion models is interesting, offering a more principled alternative to existing heuristic methods like classifier-free guidance (CFG).  Deriving correction terms for various approximations (annealed, product, geometric averaged distributions) and existing heuristics (CFG) is a valuable contribution. The SMC resampling schemes, tailored for the weighted SDEs, are also novel.
*   **Significance:** The ability to combine and control pre-trained diffusion models is a significant step.  Compositional generation and multi-objective optimization are important applications, and FKCs seem to offer a more rigorous framework than simple score mixing. The demonstration of temperature annealing for learning Boltzmann samplers is also potentially impactful, offering benefits in computational efficiency. The improved classifier-free guidance is another significant aspect. However, several factors detract from the paper's impact:
    *   The empirical results section, while demonstrating some utility, doesn't convincingly show a substantial improvement over existing methods in all cases.  The low diversity issue requiring "clipping" in image generation is concerning.
    *   The descriptions in section 5 lack clear metrics and visualizations demonstrating an improvement in the method. The image qualities with FKC seem indistinguishable. In this study, it is not clear whether FKC allows improving the prompt adherence or the image quality.
    *   The complexity of the approach could limit its adoption. The method relies on an exact score function and may not be easily applicable to all models, although the authors do give an approximate correction through Ito integration.
    *   The paper claims that the method allows sampling from a wide range of temperature values but only evaluates using tempered noise.
    *   The paper claims that the method avoids the computationally difficult task of directly training a sampler at a lower temperature. However, the figures provided in the appendix do not have the same y axes as the FKC results. As such, the claim could be stronger by comparing the computational cost of training directly at a low temperature with FKC for every benchmark.
    * The method has a higher complexity of training and therefore could detract from the usefulness of a given dataset.

**Strengths:**

*   Principled approach based on the Feynman-Kac formula.
*   Derivation of correction terms for common inference-time manipulation techniques.
*   SMC resampling algorithms tailored for weighted SDEs.
*   Demonstration of utility in diverse tasks (temperature annealing, molecule generation, CFG).

**Weaknesses:**

*   Limited, sometimes incremental, empirical gains over existing methods. The improvement in ImageReward and CLIP scores are small in the text-to-image tasks and the visualization is indistinguishable.
*   Complexity and dependence on accurate score function estimation.
*   Low diversity of samples without using clipping.
*   Unclear justification for the use of hyperparameters.
* Claims are limited and the paper lacks comparisons to baselines demonstrating direct training at low temperatures.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and theoretically sound approach to an important problem. However, the empirical evidence supporting substantial improvements is not overwhelming, and the complexity of the method could hinder its widespread adoption. Despite these limitations, the paper's potential to offer a more principled framework for controlling generative models warrants a positive, but not exceptional, score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### AlignDistil: Token-Level Language Model Alignment as Adaptive Policy Distillation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.02832v1)
- **Authors**: Songming Zhang, Xue Zhang, Tong Zhang, Bojie Hu, Yufeng Chen, Jinan Xu
- **Abstract**: In modern large language models (LLMs), LLM alignment is of crucial importance and is typically achieved through methods such as reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) and direct preference optimization (DPO). However, in most existing methods for LLM alignment, all tokens in the response are optimized using a sparse, response-level reward or preference annotation. The ignorance of token-level rewards may erroneously punish high-quality tokens or encourage low-quality tokens, resulting in suboptimal performance and slow convergence speed. To address this issue, we propose AlignDistil, an RLHF-equivalent distillation method for token-level reward optimization. Specifically, we introduce the reward learned by DPO into the RLHF objective and theoretically prove the equivalence between this objective and a token-level distillation process, where the teacher distribution linearly combines the logits from the DPO model and a reference model. On this basis, we further bridge the accuracy gap between the reward from the DPO model and the pure reward model, by building a contrastive DPO reward with a normal and a reverse DPO model. Moreover, to avoid under- and over-optimization on different tokens, we design a token adaptive logit extrapolation mechanism to construct an appropriate teacher distribution for each token. Experimental results demonstrate the superiority of our AlignDistil over existing methods and showcase fast convergence due to its token-level distributional reward optimization.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AlignDistil, a novel token-level language model alignment method that leverages adaptive policy distillation.  It addresses the limitations of existing LLM alignment methods (RLHF, DPO) that rely on sparse, response-level rewards by optimizing individual tokens. The method starts by incorporating the DPO reward into the original RLHF objective and proving a theoretical equivalence between the two at a token level. AlignDistil then introduces a contrastive DPO reward (using both normal and reverse DPO models) and a token-adaptive logit extrapolation mechanism to improve reward accuracy and avoid over/under-optimization. The authors demonstrate through experiments that AlignDistil outperforms existing methods, achieves faster convergence, and effectively optimizes based on token-level reward distributions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates solid novelty in several key aspects:

    *   **Theoretical Connection:** Establishing the theoretical equivalence between RLHF with DPO rewards and a token-level distillation process is a significant contribution. It provides a rigorous foundation for their approach.
    *   **Contrastive DPO Reward:** Using both normal and reverse DPO models is an innovative way to improve the accuracy and discriminative power of the reward function. This addresses a known weakness of standard DPO.
    *   **Token-Adaptive Logit Extrapolation:** The idea of adaptively adjusting the teacher distribution based on the disagreement between DPO and reverse DPO models for each token is a sophisticated approach to address the inherent variability and importance of different tokens.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper addresses a crucial challenge in LLM alignment: the granularity of feedback. By moving from response-level to token-level optimization, it offers a way to more precisely guide model behavior and improve convergence speed.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate the practical benefits of AlignDistil, showing improvements over established baselines on multiple benchmarks. This supports the claim that token-level distributional reward optimization is superior.
    *   The modularity and theoretical grounding make this method likely to be influential in the field.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Strong theoretical justification for the approach.
    *   Clear and well-structured presentation.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation with multiple baselines and benchmarks.
    *   Ablation studies to demonstrate the effectiveness of the individual components.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited Scale of Models Evaluated:  The experimental evaluation is primarily conducted on smaller (1.5B) language models.  The paper acknowledges this limitation.  Performance on larger models is not yet known and would provide better validation. The behavior on smaller models might not translate directly to large-scale models which can be more complex.
    *   Reliance on DPO Rewards: While the contrastive DPO addresses issues with the basic DPO reward, it’s still indirectly dependent on preference data.  The quality of the preference data used to train DPO models can significantly impact the overall effectiveness of AlignDistil.  How AlignDistil deals with noisy or biased preference data isn't thoroughly discussed.
    *   Computational Cost: Using both normal and reverse DPO potentially increases the computational cost of training. While the paper mentions the model architecture benefits in saving the required models, it does not present a detailed analysis of the overall training time and resource requirements compared to standard DPO or other baselines, especially when token-adaptive components are factored in.
    *  Limited Comparison: The experimental comparison mainly focuses on the three chosen benchmarks for LLM alignment (AlpacaEval 2.0, MT-Bench, Arena-Hard). It would be beneficial to assess the transferability of AlignDistil to other diverse datasets/tasks.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The token-level optimization approach could inspire new alignment algorithms and techniques.
    *   The theoretical framework could be extended to other forms of feedback and reward signals.
    *   The method could potentially be combined with other alignment techniques for further improvements.

**Justification for Score:**

AlignDistil represents a significant step forward in LLM alignment. It moves beyond simple response-level optimization with a theoretically sound and practically effective token-level approach. The contrastive DPO reward and adaptive logit extrapolation further enhance the method's performance and stability.  However, the limited scale of the experiments, potential reliance on clean preference data, and computational cost are areas that need further investigation.  While the innovation is strong and there are definite impacts on how we approach LLM optimization, the lack of scaling on larger LLMs prevents a score higher than 7. It needs to be evaluated at greater scale to receive higher consideration.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Mask-DPO: Generalizable Fine-grained Factuality Alignment of LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.02846v1)
- **Authors**: Yuzhe Gu, Wenwei Zhang, Chengqi Lyu, Dahua Lin, Kai Chen
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) exhibit hallucinations (i.e., unfaithful or nonsensical information) when serving as AI assistants in various domains. Since hallucinations always come with truthful content in the LLM responses, previous factuality alignment methods that conduct response-level preference learning inevitably introduced noises during training. Therefore, this paper proposes a fine-grained factuality alignment method based on Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), called Mask-DPO. Incorporating sentence-level factuality as mask signals, Mask-DPO only learns from factually correct sentences in the preferred samples and prevents the penalty on factual contents in the not preferred samples, which resolves the ambiguity in the preference learning. Extensive experimental results demonstrate that Mask-DPO can significantly improve the factuality of LLMs responses to questions from both in-domain and out-of-domain datasets, although these questions and their corresponding topics are unseen during training. Only trained on the ANAH train set, the score of Llama3.1-8B-Instruct on the ANAH test set is improved from 49.19% to 77.53%, even surpassing the score of Llama3.1-70B-Instruct (53.44%), while its FactScore on the out-of-domain Biography dataset is also improved from 30.29% to 39.39%. We further study the generalization property of Mask-DPO using different training sample scaling strategies and find that scaling the number of topics in the dataset is more effective than the number of questions. We provide a hypothesis of what factual alignment is doing with LLMs, on the implication of this phenomenon, and conduct proof-of-concept experiments to verify it. We hope the method and the findings pave the way for future research on scaling factuality alignment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Mask-DPO, a fine-grained factuality alignment method for large language models (LLMs).  Standard Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) methods can inadvertently introduce noise during training by penalizing or encouraging all sentences within a response, regardless of individual factuality. Mask-DPO addresses this by using sentence-level factuality annotations to mask out factually incorrect sentences in preferred samples and factually correct sentences in dispreferred samples, preventing incorrect signal propagation. Experiments show Mask-DPO improves factuality on both in-domain and out-of-domain datasets, surpassing other methods. The paper also explores data scaling strategies, finding that increasing the number of topics during training is more effective than increasing question diversity within topics. They hypothesize that LLMs learn a model-specific knowledge graph and factual alignment adjusts the affinity between topic nodes in this graph. Proof-of-concept experiments support this hypothesis.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of Mask-DPO, i.e. incorporating sentence-level factuality to improve preference learning, is a significant contribution. It directly tackles a key limitation of vanilla DPO in knowledge-intensive tasks. The analysis of data scaling and the knowledge graph hypothesis are also novel and interesting directions for future research. However, it builds directly upon existing DPO work.

* **Significance:** The issue of hallucination is critical for LLMs to be reliable AI assistants.  Mask-DPO demonstrably improves factuality, making it significant. The insights on data scaling offer valuable guidance for training more factual LLMs efficiently. The knowledge graph hypothesis provides a potential framework for understanding and improving LLM knowledge representation. The paper provides a solid empirical analysis on the ANAH dataset they curated but its generalizability isn't clearly established.

* **Strengths:**
    * Clear problem statement and well-motivated solution.
    * Strong empirical results demonstrating improved factuality.
    * Interesting hypothesis about the knowledge graph representation within LLMs.
    * The study on the generalization properties of Mask-DPO using different training sample scaling strategies is valuable.
    * The code release contributes to reproducibility and further research.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * Relies heavily on the ANAH dataset for evaluation, which may limit the generalizability of the findings. Expanding evaluation to other datasets would increase confidence.
    * While the knowledge graph hypothesis is interesting, the proof-of-concept experiments are somewhat limited. A deeper investigation, potentially with more direct probes of the LLM's internal representations, would strengthen the argument.
    * Some elements of the knowledge graph alignment can be seen as expected behaviors of preference learning. The extent to which these effects can be seen as unique to the alignment on knowledge is unclear.

* **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence future research on factuality alignment by:
    * Establishing sentence-level factuality as an effective signal for preference learning.
    * Encouraging further exploration of data scaling strategies for factuality alignment.
    * Inspiring research into the internal knowledge representations of LLMs.

* **Justification of Score:** The paper presents a novel method (Mask-DPO) that addresses a significant problem (hallucination) in LLMs. It provides compelling empirical results and offers a plausible and testable hypothesis. However, the evaluation is somewhat limited by its dependence on a single dataset and the depth of investigation on the knowledge graph hypothesis. Therefore, a score of 7 reflects these considerations.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Shakespearean Sparks: The Dance of Hallucination and Creativity in LLMs' Decoding Layers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.02851v1)
- **Authors**: Zicong He, Boxuan Zhang, Lu Cheng
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are known to hallucinate, a phenomenon often linked to creativity. While previous research has primarily explored this connection through theoretical or qualitative lenses, our work takes a quantitative approach to systematically examine the relationship between hallucination and creativity in LLMs. Given the complex nature of creativity, we propose a narrow definition tailored to LLMs and introduce an evaluation framework, HCL, which quantifies Hallucination and Creativity across different Layers of LLMs during decoding. Our empirical analysis reveals a tradeoff between hallucination and creativity that is consistent across layer depth, model type, and model size. Notably, across different model architectures, we identify a specific layer at each model size that optimally balances this tradeoff. Additionally, the optimal layer tends to appear in the early layers of larger models, and the confidence of the model is also significantly higher at this layer. These findings provide a quantitative perspective that offers new insights into the interplay between LLM creativity and hallucination. The code and data for our experiments are available at https://github.com/ZicongHe2002/HCL-Spark.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a framework called Hallucination and Creativity across Layers (HCL) to quantitatively analyze the relationship between hallucination and creativity in Large Language Models (LLMs) at different decoding layers. The authors propose a narrow definition of creativity tailored to LLMs and use the HCL framework to measure hallucination and creativity scores across layers, model types, and model sizes.  The experiments suggest a trade-off between hallucination and creativity, with an optimal layer at each model size that balances this trade-off. Furthermore, they find that this optimal layer tends to appear earlier in larger models, exhibiting higher confidence. The authors propose that early exiting at intermediate layers could yield better performance by balancing factual accuracy and creative output.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its quantitative analysis of the hallucination-creativity trade-off within LLMs at the layer level. While the connection between these two characteristics has been philosophically discussed (as cited), the HCL framework and its application to analyze the evolution of these traits *across layers* is a valuable contribution. The idea of early exiting based on a quantitative trade-off metric like HCB is also a novel optimization strategy.
* **Significance:** Understanding the interplay between hallucination and creativity is crucial for developing more reliable and versatile LLMs. The HCL framework offers a way to empirically assess this relationship, which can inform the design of future LLM architectures and decoding strategies. The finding that the final layer's output is not always optimal and that earlier layers might provide a better balance is a significant insight for optimizing LLM performance. Furthermore, the HCB metric provides a tool to compare the performance across layers.
* **Strengths:**
    * The HCL framework provides a concrete methodology for quantifying and analyzing the hallucination-creativity trade-off, going beyond qualitative observations.
    * The empirical analysis is reasonably thorough, covering different models, datasets, and temperature settings.
    * The concept of the "optimal layer" and its implications for early exiting is an interesting and practical finding.
    * The paper is well-structured and clearly explains the methodology and results.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * The definition of "creativity" is quite narrow and potentially limiting. It primarily focuses on diversity of *correct* answers, which might not fully capture the nuances of creativity (e.g., originality, unconventionality). It might be better to have creativity defined by novelty of response with constraints on minimum correctness scores.
    * The evaluation is primarily limited to question-answering tasks. It's unclear how well the HCL framework would generalize to more creative tasks like story generation or poetry writing.
    * Although different types of LLM sizes were used for testing, there is no rigorous analysis of the computational and algorithmic differences in the LLM's that may explain the difference in the relationship between HCB score vs. depth.
    * The ethical considerations are superficial. The "Bias & Fairness" section acknowledges the issue but does not propose any concrete solutions or address how the framework might be used to detect or mitigate bias. It could have been improved by at least providing pointers to existing mitigation strategies in literature.
    * The limitations of the paper were not addressed in the future works section (e.g. exploring a more rigorous definition of creativity.)

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on LLM architectures, decoding strategies, and evaluation methods. The HCL framework could be adopted and extended by other researchers to explore the hallucination-creativity trade-off in more detail and to develop more effective techniques for balancing these two aspects. It also offers a potentially useful tool for identifying model design issues.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and potentially significant contribution to the field of LLMs by quantitatively analyzing the hallucination-creativity trade-off across layers. The HCL framework offers a concrete methodology for assessing this relationship, and the findings regarding the "optimal layer" and early exiting are valuable insights. However, the narrow definition of creativity, the limited scope of the evaluation, and lack of a more robust ethical discussion limit the paper's overall impact.  Also, there is limited generalization and analysis to the differences in LLM architectures.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Privacy and Accuracy-Aware AI/ML Model Deduplication
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.02862v1)
- **Authors**: Hong Guan, Lei Yu, Lixi Zhou, Li Xiong, Kanchan Chowdhury, Lulu Xie, Xusheng Xiao, Jia Zou
- **Abstract**: With the growing adoption of privacy-preserving machine learning algorithms, such as Differentially Private Stochastic Gradient Descent (DP-SGD), training or fine-tuning models on private datasets has become increasingly prevalent. This shift has led to the need for models offering varying privacy guarantees and utility levels to satisfy diverse user requirements. However, managing numerous versions of large models introduces significant operational challenges, including increased inference latency, higher resource consumption, and elevated costs. Model deduplication is a technique widely used by many model serving and database systems to support high-performance and low-cost inference queries and model diagnosis queries. However, none of the existing model deduplication works has considered privacy, leading to unbounded aggregation of privacy costs for certain deduplicated models and inefficiencies when applied to deduplicate DP-trained models. We formalize the problems of deduplicating DP-trained models for the first time and propose a novel privacy- and accuracy-aware deduplication mechanism to address the problems. We developed a greedy strategy to select and assign base models to target models to minimize storage and privacy costs. When deduplicating a target model, we dynamically schedule accuracy validations and apply the Sparse Vector Technique to reduce the privacy costs associated with private validation data. Compared to baselines that do not provide privacy guarantees, our approach improved the compression ratio by up to $35\times$ for individual models (including large language models and vision transformers). We also observed up to $43\times$ inference speedup due to the reduction of I/O operations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenges of managing and serving multiple differentially private (DP) AI/ML models, which are increasingly prevalent due to data privacy regulations.  Managing many models with varying privacy budgets leads to increased storage costs, inference latency, and resource consumption.  The paper proposes a privacy- and accuracy-aware model deduplication mechanism to address these issues.  The key contributions include: (1) formalizing the problem of model deduplication with privacy constraints, (2) developing a base model selection strategy to minimize storage and privacy costs, (3) creating dynamic block deduplication algorithms that balance validation frequency and rollback costs, and (4) using the Sparse Vector Technique (SVT) for efficient accuracy validation using private data.  Experimental results demonstrate significant improvements in compression ratio, inference speedup, and reduced privacy costs compared to baseline methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in explicitly addressing privacy concerns within the context of model deduplication. Existing model deduplication techniques do not account for the impact of deduplication on the overall privacy budget of DP models, which this paper remedies. The formulation of the privacy-aware deduplication problem is a significant contribution. The integration of SVT for private validation is also a novel element.
*   **Significance:** This paper is highly significant for a number of reasons. DP is rapidly becoming more widespread and is going to have more and more of an impact on the machine learning field, so tools and technologies that deal with privacy budgets efficiently will become increasingly valuable. Model deduplication is a well-established technique for reducing storage and improving performance, but incorporating privacy considerations is essential for DP models. The proposed approach offers a practical way to manage large collections of DP models, potentially enabling wider adoption of privacy-preserving machine learning. The observed gains in compression ratio and inference speedup are substantial, indicating the practical value of the proposed techniques.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The problem is clearly defined and well-motivated by real-world applications like model marketplaces and MLaaS platforms.
    *   The privacy budget derivation is rigorous and provides a solid theoretical foundation.
    *   The base model selection strategy balances privacy and compression costs effectively.
    *   The dynamic deduplication algorithms are designed to manage the trade-offs between accuracy validation frequency and rollback costs.
    *   The integration of SVT for private accuracy validation is a crucial element for protecting sensitive data.
    *   The experimental evaluation is comprehensive, covering a diverse range of model architectures and tasks.
    *   The ablation studies provide insights into the effectiveness of individual components.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper focuses primarily on epsilon (ε) and could discuss delta (δ) more thoroughly.
    *   The experimental results are specific to the chosen datasets and model architectures. While the results are promising, it would be helpful to investigate the sensitivity of the proposed techniques to different data distributions and model characteristics.
    *   The greedy nature of the base model selection strategy might not always yield the globally optimal solution. Exploring alternative optimization techniques could lead to further improvements. The assumption that each target model uses a single base model is a simplification that limits the compression possibilities.
    *   While the results indicate the promise for real-world DP-ML systems, the scale of these real-world systems is still small. The practical performance and cost savings of deduplication on a truly large-scale system remain to be demonstrated.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to influence the design and implementation of model serving platforms for DP models.  The proposed techniques can be incorporated into existing systems to improve storage efficiency, reduce inference latency, and manage privacy budgets. This can facilitate wider adoption of private AI/ML in various applications.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:** The paper presents a significant and novel contribution to the field of privacy-preserving machine learning. The formulation of the privacy-aware model deduplication problem and the development of practical techniques to address it are highly valuable. The comprehensive experimental evaluation demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed approach. However, the limitations related to data distributions, model characteristics, and the greedy base model selection strategy prevent a higher score. It's an impressive work that makes a clear contribution to the field, justifying the high score, but some areas could be refined.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Calibrating LLM Confidence with Semantic Steering: A Multi-Prompt Aggregation Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.02863v1)
- **Authors**: Ziang Zhou, Tianyuan Jin, Jieming Shi, Qing Li
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) often exhibit misaligned confidence scores, usually overestimating the reliability of their predictions. While verbalized confidence in Large Language Models (LLMs) has gained attention, prior work remains divided on whether confidence scores can be systematically steered through prompting. Recent studies even argue that such prompt-induced confidence shifts are negligible, suggesting LLMs' confidence calibration is rigid to linguistic interventions. Contrary to these claims, we first rigorously confirm the existence of directional confidence shifts by probing three models (including GPT3.5, LLAMA3-70b, GPT4) across 7 benchmarks, demonstrating that explicit instructions can inflate or deflate confidence scores in a regulated manner. Based on this observation, we propose a novel framework containing three components: confidence steering, steered confidence aggregation and steered answers selection, named SteeringConf. Our method, SteeringConf, leverages a confidence manipulation mechanism to steer the confidence scores of LLMs in several desired directions, followed by a summarization module that aggregates the steered confidence scores to produce a final prediction. We evaluate our method on 7 benchmarks and it consistently outperforms the baselines in terms of calibration metrics in task of confidence calibration and failure detection.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, including a novelty/significance score.

**Summary**

The paper introduces SteeringConf, a novel framework aimed at improving the confidence calibration of Large Language Models (LLMs).  The core idea is to steer the confidence scores of LLMs by using prompts with varying degrees of confidence-related instructions (e.g., "Be very cautious," "Be confident").  The framework consists of three components: (1) *Confidence Steering*, which uses these prompts to generate multiple predictions and confidence scores; (2) *Steered Confidence Aggregation*, which combines the steered confidence scores based on answer consistency and confidence reliability; and (3) *Steered Answer Selection*, which chooses the answer associated with the best calibrated confidence. The authors demonstrate through experiments on seven benchmarks and three LLMs (GPT-3.5, LLaMA3-70B, GPT-4) that SteeringConf outperforms existing calibration methods in terms of both confidence calibration metrics (ECE) and failure detection (AUROC). The paper challenges the notion that LLM calibration is inherently rigid and proposes a practical method for improving trustworthyness.

**Critical Evaluation**

*Novelty:*

The paper's novelty lies in the systematic approach of SteeringConf and the counter-intuitive finding that LLM confidence scores can be directionally steered through prompt engineering. While prompting strategies have been explored for various LLM tasks, their application to directly manipulate and calibrate confidence is less common, especially with such a controlled and aggregated approach.  The three components, confidence steering, steered confidence aggregation and steered answer selection, taken together, appear to be a novel contribution.  The detailed analysis of confidence steering effects (conservative vs. boosting) provides valuable insights into how different prompts impact LLM behavior. The analysis regarding the ordering of elicitating answer first vs confidence first is also noteworthy.

*Significance:*

Improving the confidence calibration of LLMs is a crucial problem for their safe and reliable deployment in high-stakes applications. Overconfident predictions can lead to detrimental outcomes, while a well-calibrated model can signal its uncertainty and allow for human intervention or alternative strategies. SteeringConf provides a practical and relatively easy-to-implement approach to address this challenge. The consistent improvements observed across multiple benchmarks and LLMs suggest the potential of SteeringConf to enhance the trustworthiness of these models. The findings of confidence steering provides a more in-depth exploration of LLM behavior, which would be valuable for downstream research.

*Strengths:*

*   **Strong Empirical Validation:**  The extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks and LLMs provide strong evidence supporting the effectiveness of SteeringConf.
*   **Clear Problem Definition and Solution:**  The paper clearly articulates the problem of misaligned confidence calibration and presents a well-defined and understandable framework as a solution.
*   **Insightful Analysis:** The analysis of different steering strategies and their impact on confidence metrics provides valuable insights for future research.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Reliance on Manual Prompt Engineering:** The framework's reliance on manually designed steering prompts is a significant limitation. This introduces a scalability challenge, especially for extending the framework to more granular steering directions. Furthermore, what's the best prompts might vary per task or data distributions.
*   **Computational Cost:** The requirement for multiple forward passes through the LLM increases the computational cost.
*   **Limited Theoretical Justification:** While the empirical results are compelling, the paper lacks a strong theoretical justification for why the proposed approach works. Deeper analysis from the perspective of LLMs' inner working could provide more insights.
*   **Limited generalizability:** The authors conducted their experiment on 3 LLMs and 7 datasets. It would strengthen the paper if the authors could provide analysis/results on more datasets and LLMs.

*Potential Influence:*

SteeringConf has the potential to influence the field by:

*   **Inspiring further research on confidence steering:** The paper could motivate researchers to explore automated prompt generation techniques for confidence steering and investigate the theoretical underpinnings of this phenomenon.
*   **Improving LLM trustworthiness:** By providing a practical method for improving confidence calibration, SteeringConf could contribute to the more widespread and reliable deployment of LLMs.
*   **Serving as a baseline for future calibration methods:**  The framework could serve as a strong baseline for evaluating new confidence calibration approaches.

*Overall Score & Justification:*

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses of the paper, I assign a score of **7.5**. The paper introduces a novel and practical framework for improving LLM confidence calibration, supported by strong empirical evidence. However, the reliance on manual prompt engineering and the increased computational cost limit its scalability. The score is a reflection of the paper's valuable contribution, tempered by its limitations. Furthermore, LLMs are rapidly changing, the results are highly dependent on the specific LLM/setting, thus future research would be needed to validate the findings.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### FairSense-AI: Responsible AI Meets Sustainability
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.02865v2)
- **Authors**: Shaina Raza, Mukund Sayeeganesh Chettiar, Matin Yousefabadi, Tahniat Khan, Marcelo Lotif
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we introduce FairSense-AI: a multimodal framework designed to detect and mitigate bias in both text and images. By leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs), FairSense-AI uncovers subtle forms of prejudice or stereotyping that can appear in content, providing users with bias scores, explanatory highlights, and automated recommendations for fairness enhancements. In addition, FairSense-AI integrates an AI risk assessment component that aligns with frameworks like the MIT AI Risk Repository and NIST AI Risk Management Framework, enabling structured identification of ethical and safety concerns. The platform is optimized for energy efficiency via techniques such as model pruning and mixed-precision computation, thereby reducing its environmental footprint. Through a series of case studies and applications, we demonstrate how FairSense-AI promotes responsible AI use by addressing both the social dimension of fairness and the pressing need for sustainability in large-scale AI deployments. https://vectorinstitute.github.io/FairSense-AI, https://pypi.org/project/fair-sense-ai/ (Sustainability , Responsible AI , Large Language Models , Vision Language Models , Ethical AI , Green AI)
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FairSense-AI, a multimodal framework designed to detect and mitigate bias in both text and images. It leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs) to identify subtle forms of prejudice, providing bias scores, explanations, and recommendations for fairness enhancements. The framework also integrates an AI risk assessment component aligned with frameworks like MIT's AI Risk Repository and NIST's AI Risk Management Framework. Furthermore, FairSense-AI emphasizes sustainability by optimizing for energy efficiency through model pruning and mixed-precision computation. The paper presents case studies and applications to demonstrate the framework's ability to promote responsible AI by addressing both social fairness and environmental concerns.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its integration of several existing ideas into a cohesive framework. While LLMs and VLMs are already employed for bias detection, and frameworks for AI risk assessment exist, the combination of these with an emphasis on sustainability (Green AI principles) is a unique selling point. However, the individual components are not groundbreaking, and the integration might not be technically deep, potentially relying on straightforward application of existing APIs. It expands on the author's previous work "UnBias" by including images, however, it is unclear how the integration is realized.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a relevant and timely problem – the need for responsible AI that is both fair and sustainable. Algorithmic bias and the environmental impact of large models are two of the most pressing concerns in the AI field today. By providing a practical tool to address these issues, FairSense-AI has the potential to make a significant contribution. The tool appears to be user-friendly and offers explanations of biases and risk management recommendations.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Focus:** FairSense-AI is presented as a practical, usable tool with a clear pipeline and a user interface, making it potentially attractive to practitioners.
    *   **Multimodal Approach:** The ability to analyze both text and images for bias is a significant strength, reflecting the multimodal nature of much of modern content.
    *   **Integration of Risk Assessment and Sustainability:** The incorporation of AI risk assessment frameworks and Green AI principles is forward-thinking and aligns with emerging ethical and environmental considerations.
    *   **Explanations and Recommendations:** The framework provides explanations for identified biases and offers recommendations for mitigation, making it more useful and educational than simple bias detection tools.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Technical Depth:** The paper could benefit from more technical detail on how the LLMs and VLMs are used for bias detection, how the models are fine-tuned for bias identification, and how the mixed-precision computation and model pruning are implemented.
    *   **Lack of Evaluation:** The evaluation section lacks concrete metrics (e.g., precision, recall, F1-score) to quantify the effectiveness of bias detection and mitigation. The examples provided are illustrative, but not statistically significant. It is also unclear how much the fine-tuning of the models improves the overall performance.
    *   **Reliance on Existing Models:** The framework's performance is dependent on the quality and biases of the underlying LLMs and VLMs, which could limit its effectiveness and require careful monitoring and adaptation.
    *   **Subjectivity of Fairness:** The definition of fairness is inherently subjective and context-dependent. The paper acknowledges this, but it could further discuss how the framework addresses this challenge, perhaps through user customization or the integration of diverse perspectives.
    *   **Sustainability Claims**: There is lack of numerical results on reduction of CO2 emission after applying model pruning, mixed-precision computation and targeted fine-tuning.
    *   **AI Risk Management Tool**: How it leverages a sentence transformer model and FAISS indexes requires further elaboration.

*   **Potential Influence:** FairSense-AI has the potential to influence the field by providing a concrete example of how to integrate fairness and sustainability into AI development. If widely adopted, it could raise awareness of these issues and encourage more responsible AI practices.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper presents a valuable framework for addressing bias and promoting sustainability in AI. Its strengths lie in its practical focus, multimodal approach, and integration of risk assessment and Green AI principles. However, the paper's novelty is somewhat limited by its reliance on existing techniques, and the evaluation is weak, lacking quantitative metrics. The limitations surrounding the definition of fairness and relying on existing models should be carefully addressed. The current lack of numerical results with respect to sustainability is concerning.

**Score: 6**

While the paper addresses a relevant and timely problem with a well-structured framework, it does not present significant technical breakthroughs. The paper needs stronger empirical validation and more details on its technical underpinnings to reach a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Prompting Generative AI with Interaction-Augmented Instructions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.02874v1)
- **Authors**: Leixian Shen, Haotian Li, Yifang Wang, Xing Xie, Huamin Qu
- **Abstract**: The emergence of generative AI (GenAI) models, including large language models and text-to-image models, has significantly advanced the synergy between humans and AI with not only their outstanding capability but more importantly, the intuitive communication method with text prompts. Though intuitive, text-based instructions suffer from natural languages' ambiguous and redundant nature.
