### The Devil Is in the Details: Tackling Unimodal Spurious Correlations for Generalizable Multimodal Reward Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03122v1)
- **Authors**: Zichao Li, Xueru Wen, Jie Lou, Yuqiu Ji, Yaojie Lu, Xianpei Han, Debing Zhang, Le Sun
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Reward Models (MM-RMs) are crucial for aligning Large Language Models (LLMs) with human preferences, particularly as LLMs increasingly interact with multimodal data. However, we find that MM-RMs trained on existing datasets often struggle to generalize to out-of-distribution data due to their reliance on unimodal spurious correlations, primarily text-only shortcuts within the training distribution, which prevents them from leveraging true multimodal reward functions. To address this, we introduce a Shortcut-aware MM-RM learning algorithm that mitigates this issue by dynamically reweighting training samples, shifting the distribution toward better multimodal understanding, and reducing dependence on unimodal spurious correlations. Our experiments demonstrate significant improvements in generalization, downstream task performance, and scalability, establishing a more robust framework for multimodal reward modeling.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "The Devil Is in the Details: Tackling Unimodal Spurious Correlations for Generalizable Multimodal Reward Models" investigates the generalization limitations of Multimodal Reward Models (MM-RMs) in aligning Large Language Models (LLMs) with human preferences. The authors identify a key issue: MM-RMs tend to rely on unimodal (text-only) spurious correlations present in training data, hindering their ability to generalize to out-of-distribution (OOD) data. To address this, they propose a "Shortcut-aware MM-RM" learning algorithm that dynamically reweights training samples to mitigate reliance on these spurious correlations and encourage true multimodal understanding. Experiments demonstrate improved generalization, downstream task performance, and scalability using the proposed method.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The identification of unimodal spurious correlations as a significant obstacle to MM-RM generalization is a valuable contribution. While spurious correlations are a known issue in machine learning, the paper specifically highlights and characterizes their impact on the *multimodal* reward modeling context. The proposed Shortcut-aware MM-RM learning algorithm is also novel. It's not simply a re-weighting scheme, but it's designed to leverage a text-only proxy model to explicitly identify and address instances where unimodal shortcuts fail. This explicit approach offers a targeted solution.
*   **Significance:** MM-RMs are crucial for aligning LLMs, particularly as they interact with multimodal data. Improving their generalization capabilities is, therefore, a highly significant goal. The paper demonstrates tangible improvements in OOD generalization and downstream task performance using the proposed method. If the method proves robust across a broader range of datasets and model architectures, it could have a substantial impact on the reliability and trustworthiness of aligned LLMs. The proposed Short-Failure Degradation metric also provides a valuable diagnostic tool for analyzing and understanding the generalization behavior of MM-RMs.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of unimodal spurious correlations and their impact on MM-RM generalization.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:** The paper presents a rigorous analysis of the issue, including empirical evidence and the introduction of the SFD metric.
    *   **Targeted Solution:** The Shortcut-aware MM-RM learning algorithm is specifically designed to address the identified problem.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed method, demonstrating improvements in OOD generalization and downstream task performance.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Dataset Diversity:** The experiments are conducted on a relatively small set of three vision-language preference datasets. While the datasets vary in construction method, further testing across a more diverse range of datasets and modalities would strengthen the claims of generalizability.
    *   **Text-Only Focus:** While the focus on text-only shortcuts is valuable, it's possible that other types of unimodal or even bimodal spurious correlations exist. Addressing these in future work could lead to further improvements in MM-RM generalization.
    *   **Incremental Improvement on i.i.d.:** Although designed to mitigate OOD error, a more through justification is needed on why there's a slight trade-off with i.i.d (in-distribution) performance to show a good rationale when choosing to use the method.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by raising awareness of the importance of addressing unimodal spurious correlations in MM-RM development. The proposed Shortcut-aware MM-RM learning algorithm provides a practical approach for improving generalization and could inspire further research in this area. The SFD metric offers a valuable tool for analyzing and comparing the generalization behavior of different MM-RMs.

**Overall:** The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of multimodal reward modeling by identifying and addressing the problem of unimodal spurious correlations. The proposed Shortcut-aware MM-RM learning algorithm offers a promising approach for improving generalization, and the SFD metric provides a useful tool for analysis. Despite some limitations in dataset diversity and the potential for further research into other types of spurious correlations, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses, making it a significant contribution.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards Understanding Multi-Round Large Language Model Reasoning: Approximability, Learnability and Generalizability
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03128v1)
- **Authors**: Chenhui Xu, Dancheng Liu, Jiajie Li, Amir Nassereldine, Zhaohui Li, Jinjun Xiong
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in cognitive science and multi-round reasoning techniques for Large Language Models (LLMs) suggest that iterative thinking processes improve problem-solving performance in complex tasks. Inspired by this, approaches like Chain-of-Thought, debating, and self-refinement have been applied to auto-regressive LLMs, achieving significant successes in tasks such as mathematical reasoning, commonsense reasoning, and multi-hop question answering. Despite these successes, the theoretical basis for how multi-round reasoning enhances problem-solving abilities remains underexplored. In this work, we investigate the approximation, learnability, and generalization properties of multi-round auto-regressive models. We show that Transformers with finite context windows are universal approximators for steps of Turing-computable functions and can approximate any Turing-computable sequence-to-sequence function through multi-round reasoning. We extend PAC learning to sequence generation and demonstrate that multi-round generation is learnable even when the sequence length exceeds the model's context window. Finally, we examine how generalization error propagates across rounds, and show how the aforementioned approaches can help constrain this error, ensuring outputs stay within an expectation boundary. This work sheds light on the systemic theoretical foundations of multi-round sequence learning and reasoning, emphasizing its role in inference complexity.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the theoretical properties (approximability, learnability, and generalizability) of multi-round reasoning with large language models (LLMs) that use auto-regressive architectures with limited context windows. It shows that Transformers with finite context are universal approximators for steps of Turing-computable functions, and that multi-round reasoning can approximate any Turing-computable sequence-to-sequence function. It extends PAC learning to sequence generation, demonstrating learnability even when sequence length exceeds the context window.  Finally, it analyzes how generalization error propagates across rounds and suggests how techniques like Chain-of-Thought and self-refinement can constrain this error. The work aims to provide a theoretical foundation for understanding why multi-round reasoning enhances problem-solving abilities in LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper attempts to provide a solid theoretical grounding for the empirical success of multi-round reasoning techniques in LLMs. While the individual components of the analysis (Turing machine approximation, PAC learning extension, error propagation) are established areas, the *combination* of these frameworks to analyze multi-round reasoning specifically *is a notable contribution*.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Framework:** The paper provides a comprehensive analysis by addressing approximation, learnability, and generalization within a single framework. This is valuable as these aspects are often studied independently.
*   **Relevant to Practice:** The focus on finite context windows makes the theoretical results more directly relevant to real-world LLM implementations.
*   **Formal Guarantees:** The paper provides formal approximation, learnability and generalization guarantees, rather than solely relying on empirical observations.
*   **Explaining Empirical Success:** The paper attempts to explain the empirical success of existing prompting strategies (CoT, self-refinement) by linking them to error constraint during inference.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Simplifying Assumptions:** The theoretical analysis necessarily relies on simplifying assumptions (e.g., Lipschitz continuity, bounded norms, error models) that might not perfectly reflect the behavior of real-world LLMs. The gap between these assumptions and practical LLMs needs more explicit discussion.
*   **Limited Novelty of Individual Components:** While the combination is novel, some of the individual theoretical components (e.g., transformer approximation) are based on existing results. The primary contribution lies in how these are combined and applied to the multi-round reasoning setting.
*   **High Level Abstraction:** The analysis remains somewhat abstract. It would be stronger if it could generate concrete and testable predictions about the sample complexity of different multi-round reasoning techniques under specific conditions, and demonstrate the predictions by experiment. The theoretical analysis also needs more explicit connection to existing LLM architectures.

**Significance:**

The paper is significant because it moves beyond purely empirical evaluations of multi-round reasoning and attempts to establish a principled theoretical understanding. By grounding the observed benefits in approximation, learnability, and generalization, the paper opens avenues for more informed model design and training strategies for these techniques. However, its impact hinges on the accuracy of its assumptions and the ability to translate the abstract results into actionable insights.  While it explains, at a high level, how multi-round reasoning methods may constraint errors during generation, it lacks a rigorous experimental validation to verify the hypothesis and how it impacts the empirical performance of the techniques.

The main limitation of the work lies in the limited novelty of individual components and high abstraction of the proposed model, which requires significant simplification and assumptions, limiting its practical contribution.

**Score: 6**

**Justification:**

A score of 6 reflects a solid, but not groundbreaking contribution. The paper makes a valuable attempt to provide a theoretical underpinning for an important practical problem (multi-round reasoning). It is not a game changer and requires more concrete demonstration to impact how the multi-round reasoning techniques are developed in practice.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Bridging Molecular Graphs and Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03135v1)
- **Authors**: Runze Wang, Mingqi Yang, Yanming Shen
- **Abstract**: While Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown exceptional generalization capabilities, their ability to process graph data, such as molecular structures, remains limited. To bridge this gap, this paper proposes Graph2Token, an efficient solution that aligns graph tokens to LLM tokens. The key idea is to represent a graph token with the LLM token vocabulary, without fine-tuning the LLM backbone. To achieve this goal, we first construct a molecule-text paired dataset from multisources, including CHEBI and HMDB, to train a graph structure encoder, which reduces the distance between graphs and texts representations in the feature space. Then, we propose a novel alignment strategy that associates a graph token with LLM tokens. To further unleash the potential of LLMs, we collect molecular IUPAC name identifiers, which are incorporated into the LLM prompts. By aligning molecular graphs as special tokens, we can activate LLM generalization ability to molecular few-shot learning. Extensive experiments on molecular classification and regression tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of our proposed Graph2Token.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Bridging Molecular Graphs and Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Graph2Token, a method for enabling Large Language Models (LLMs) to process molecular graph data effectively.  The core idea is to represent molecular graphs as special tokens that are aligned with the LLM's existing vocabulary, avoiding the need for fine-tuning the LLM backbone. This alignment is achieved by pre-training a graph encoder using a molecular-text dataset (from sources like CHEBI and HMDB) and then learning a graph tokenizer to map graph features to LLM token embeddings.  The approach incorporates IUPAC names within the LLM prompts to leverage existing knowledge.  The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of Graph2Token on molecular classification and regression tasks, especially in few-shot learning scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to incorporating graph data into LLMs without fine-tuning the LLM's parameters.  The idea of learning a graph token within the LLM's existing vocabulary is a promising concept. The use of a multi-source molecular-text dataset and the incorporation of IUPAC names are helpful data engineering aspects. The technique differs from many existing approaches that either convert graphs to text or rely on fine-tuning.
*   **Significance:** The ability to process molecular graphs effectively opens up possibilities for applying LLMs to a wide range of molecular tasks, including drug discovery, materials science, and chemical synthesis.  The authors' focus on few-shot learning is significant because it addresses the data scarcity often encountered in biomolecular domains.  The claimed preservation of the LLM's pre-trained knowledge is a potentially important advantage over fine-tuning methods.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The approach avoids fine-tuning the LLM, potentially preserving its generalization ability and reducing computational cost.
    *   The design leverages existing knowledge within LLMs by using IUPAC names in prompts.
    *   The experiments show competitive performance in few-shot learning, a crucial setting for molecular tasks.
    *   The ablation study provides insights into the contribution of different components of Graph2Token.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The gains over the best baselines, while present, are not overwhelmingly large in all experiments.
    *   While it's positive that the paper avoids modifying the LLM weights, it still requires training the graph encoder and the tokenizer. The amount of resources involved in training these components should be clarified.
    *   A thorough analysis of failure cases and limitations of Graph2Token would strengthen the paper. For example, when might this approach struggle? How does the size of the LLM vocabulary affect performance?
    *   While the description of Graph2Token is clear, more details about the training process of the graph encoder and the graph tokenizer could be helpful.  What loss functions are used?  How is the training data curated?
    *   It would be beneficial to explore the interpretability of the learned graph tokens. Can we gain any insights into what aspects of the graph structure the LLM focuses on?

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence research in graph machine learning and its application to molecular problems.  The Graph2Token approach could inspire new methods for incorporating structured data into LLMs without compromising their pre-trained knowledge.
*   **Rigour and Evidence:** The experiments are well-designed and cover a range of molecular tasks. The baselines are appropriate, and the ablation study is informative. The number of parameters is small in comparison to the baseline with LoRA. However, comparing Graph2Token with methods that use textual embeddings (e.g., SMILES string) without finetuning can improve the experimental study.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**
The paper presents a novel and significant approach to incorporating molecular graph data into LLMs. It addresses a crucial limitation of existing methods by avoiding fine-tuning and aims to preserve the LLM's pre-trained knowledge. The experiments provide solid evidence for the effectiveness of Graph2Token in few-shot learning. However, the gains are not always dramatic, and more detailed analyses and further investigation are required to maximize the impact of this method.

The overall contributions are deemed of sufficient significance to warrant publication and further study within the field.

It should be noted that the high assigned score means the method does show promising results while the overall contributions need to be more explored and refined.
- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Implicit U-KAN2.0: Dynamic, Efficient and Interpretable Medical Image Segmentation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03141v1)
- **Authors**: Chun-Wun Cheng, Yining Zhao, Yanqi Cheng, Javier Montoya, Carola-Bibiane Schönlieb, Angelica I Aviles-Rivero
- **Abstract**: Image segmentation is a fundamental task in both image analysis and medical applications. State-of-the-art methods predominantly rely on encoder-decoder architectures with a U-shaped design, commonly referred to as U-Net. Recent advancements integrating transformers and MLPs improve performance but still face key limitations, such as poor interpretability, difficulty handling intrinsic noise, and constrained expressiveness due to discrete layer structures, often lacking a solid theoretical foundation.In this work, we introduce Implicit U-KAN 2.0, a novel U-Net variant that adopts a two-phase encoder-decoder structure. In the SONO phase, we use a second-order neural ordinary differential equation (NODEs), called the SONO block, for a more efficient, expressive, and theoretically grounded modeling approach. In the SONO-MultiKAN phase, we integrate the second-order NODEs and MultiKAN layer as the core computational block to enhance interpretability and representation power. Our contributions are threefold. First, U-KAN 2.0 is an implicit deep neural network incorporating MultiKAN and second order NODEs, improving interpretability and performance while reducing computational costs. Second, we provide a theoretical analysis demonstrating that the approximation ability of the MultiKAN block is independent of the input dimension. Third, we conduct extensive experiments on a variety of 2D and a single 3D dataset, demonstrating that our model consistently outperforms existing segmentation networks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Implicit U-KAN 2.0, a novel U-Net architecture for medical image segmentation. The key innovations lie in the use of Second-Order Neural Ordinary Differential Equations (SONO) blocks and SONO-MultiKAN blocks within a U-Net framework. SONO blocks aim to provide a more efficient and theoretically sound way to model feature evolution in a continuous manner, leading to faster convergence and improved stability. The SONO-MultiKAN blocks integrate these SONO blocks with MultiKAN layers to enhance interpretability and representation power.  The authors claim improved performance, efficiency, and interpretability compared to existing segmentation networks. They theoretically analyze the MultiKAN block's approximation capabilities and experimentally demonstrate superior performance on several 2D and one 3D medical image segmentation datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty of Architecture:** The combination of second-order NODEs with MultiKAN layers within a U-Net framework is a relatively novel approach. The SONO block aims to address the limitations of discrete layer structures in traditional CNNs and Transformers by modeling continuous feature evolution.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides a theoretical analysis (Kolmogorov Arnold theorem for MultiKAN) to support the approximation ability of the MultiKAN block, independent of input dimensionality.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results demonstrate consistent performance improvements over several baseline and state-of-the-art methods across multiple datasets, including both 2D and 3D images. The results are quantified using standard segmentation metrics like Dice score, HD95, IoU, and F1 score.
    *   **Addressing Limitations of Prior Work:** The paper explicitly aims to address the limitations of U-KAN (which relies on addition only and lacks full GPU compatibility) by incorporating multiplication and optimizing for GPU-based training.
    *   **Improved Robustness:** The ablation studies highlight improved robustness to noise compared to existing methods. This is a crucial factor in medical image segmentation due to inherent image quality variations.
    *   **Constant Memory:** The claims of maintaining constant memory costs are significant, especially for GPU-intensive tasks.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Interpretability Claims:** While the authors claim improved interpretability due to the MultiKAN layer, the extent to which this interpretability is practically useful or accessible is not thoroughly demonstrated.  The paper doesn't provide specific examples or methods for extracting and interpreting the learned activation functions in MultiKAN to gain insights into the segmentation process. It would significantly strengthen the work to showcase how MultiKAN facilitates understanding feature selection or error analysis.
    *   **Limited Datasets**: Using only one 3D medical imaging dataset is not enough to establish the broad effectiveness of the model in 3D cases. More diverse 3D experiments across different body regions and modalities would provide a more comprehensive evaluation.
    *   **Ablation Study Scope**: While there's an ablation study on noise, more detailed ablation studies dissecting the contributions of the individual components (SONO, MultiKAN, bottleneck module, skip connections) would enhance understanding of the architecture's effectiveness.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The paper could benefit from including more detailed implementation information or publicly releasing the code to facilitate reproducibility. Although authors claim that the model is GPU-based, they do not provide enough details to allow other researchers reproduce the results.
    *   **Comparison to recent models:** The method fails to compare to the most recent segmentation models, which casts a concern on the true advance of the method.

*   **Significance:**

    The paper contributes a potentially valuable architecture for medical image segmentation by addressing limitations in existing methods. The integration of continuous modeling via SONO blocks and the increased representation power of MultiKAN layers offer a promising avenue for future research. The claims of improved robustness to noise are particularly relevant in clinical settings. If the promised improvements in efficiency and GPU compatibility can be replicated, this work could have a practical impact.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel architecture with a clear motivation, theoretical grounding, and compelling experimental results.  However, the claims of improved interpretability need to be better substantiated. The limited scope of the 3D experiments and the absence of certain ablation studies are also drawbacks. The absence of comparisons with more recent state-of-the-art methods is concerning. Taking all these factors into account, the paper merits a score that recognizes its contributions but also acknowledges areas for improvement.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 6/10

### PriFFT: Privacy-preserving Federated Fine-tuning of Large Language Models via Function Secret Sharing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03146v1)
- **Authors**: Zhichao You, Xuewen Dong, Ke Cheng, Xutong Mu, Jiaxuan Fu, Shiyang Ma, Qiang Qu, Yulong Shen
- **Abstract**: Fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) raises privacy concerns due to the risk of exposing sensitive training data. Federated learning (FL) mitigates this risk by keeping training samples on local devices, but recent studies show that adversaries can still infer private information from model updates in FL. Additionally, LLM parameters are typically shared publicly during federated fine-tuning, while developers are often reluctant to disclose these parameters, posing further security challenges. Inspired by the above problems, we propose PriFFT, a privacy-preserving federated fine-tuning mechanism, to protect both the model updates and parameters. In PriFFT, clients and the server share model inputs and parameters by secret sharing, performing secure fine-tuning on shared values without accessing plaintext data. Due to considerable LLM parameters, privacy-preserving federated fine-tuning invokes complex secure calculations and requires substantial communication and computation resources. To optimize the efficiency of privacy-preserving federated fine-tuning of LLMs, we introduce function secret-sharing protocols for various operations, including reciprocal calculation, tensor products, natural exponentiation, softmax, hyperbolic tangent, and dropout. The proposed protocols achieve up to 4.02X speed improvement and reduce 7.19X communication overhead compared to the implementation based on existing secret sharing methods. Besides, PriFFT achieves a 2.23X speed improvement and reduces 4.08X communication overhead in privacy-preserving fine-tuning without accuracy drop compared to the existing secret sharing methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "PriFFT: Privacy-preserving Federated Fine-tuning of Large Language Models via Function Secret Sharing":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PriFFT, a privacy-preserving federated fine-tuning mechanism for large language models (LLMs) that aims to protect both model updates and model parameters.  PriFFT employs function secret sharing (FSS) to share model inputs and parameters between clients and the server, enabling secure fine-tuning without exposing plaintext data. To address the computational complexity associated with large LLMs, the paper proposes optimized FSS-based protocols for various operations (reciprocal calculation, tensor products, etc.).  The authors claim significant speed improvements and reduced communication overhead compared to implementations using existing secret sharing methods like ABY2, while maintaining comparable model accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using function secret sharing for privacy-preserving federated fine-tuning of LLMs has some novelty, but not groundbreaking. Prior work already addresses privacy issues in FL. The main contribution here is the application and optimization of FSS specifically to LLM fine-tuning and the design of FSS-friendly protocols for common LLM operations. This optimization targeting LLMs is where the main novelty lies, particularly in tailoring FSS techniques for the specific computations involved.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a relevant and important problem: the need for privacy in federated fine-tuning of LLMs. Protecting both model updates and parameters is a crucial step toward more secure and trustworthy federated learning systems. The efficiency improvements claimed through FSS-based protocols are significant, as they could make privacy-preserving LLM fine-tuning more practical. If those improvements do as they claim, that means that the work is well done and achieves what it sets out to do in a satisfactory way. However, the paper needs to do a better job at providing concrete advantages in a practical application or some tangible real-world result.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the privacy risks in federated LLM fine-tuning, covering both data leakage from model updates and the exposure of model parameters.
    *   **Technical Contribution:** The design of optimized FSS-based protocols for LLM-specific operations (tensor products, exponentiation, softmax, etc.) is a significant technical contribution.
    *   **Performance Evaluation:** The paper provides empirical results comparing PriFFT to ABY2, demonstrating claimed speed improvements and reduced communication overhead.
    *   **Completeness:** The paper presents both theoretical analysis (security proofs) and experimental validation, strengthening its claims.
    *   **Writing Quality:** Generally, the paper is well-written and organized.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the application of FSS is novel to *this specific problem*, FSS itself is not a new technique, and the core optimization strategies may be incremental.
    *   **Limited Generalization:** The specific optimizations in PriFFT are highly tailored to current LLM architectures and may not generalize well to future LLMs with different computational characteristics.
    *   **Real-world Experiments Lacking:** The experiments are conducted in a controlled LAN environment.  A stronger evaluation would involve experiments in more realistic federated learning settings with variable network conditions and heterogeneous clients. The paper could also demonstrate some positive results in a concrete real-world application to better demonstrate the value proposition of the technology.
    *   **Accuracy Drop:** While claiming comparable accuracy, the paper does report some accuracy drop compared to plaintext fine-tuning. A more detailed analysis of the trade-offs between privacy and accuracy is needed.
    *   **Security Assumptions:** The security analysis assumes semi-honest adversaries. While standard, this is a strong assumption.  Discussion of robustness against malicious adversaries would be valuable.

*   **Potential Influence:**  If the claimed performance improvements hold in more realistic settings, PriFFT could influence the adoption of privacy-preserving techniques in federated LLM fine-tuning. The FSS-based protocols could be incorporated into existing federated learning frameworks. This work is also not too difficult to extend so there can be other improvements made by future research.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a technically sound and well-evaluated system for privacy-preserving federated fine-tuning of LLMs. While the core idea builds upon existing techniques (FSS and federated learning), the specific optimizations tailored to LLM computations contribute significant improvements. The limitations regarding generalization and real-world deployment prevent a higher score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### DSVD: Dynamic Self-Verify Decoding for Faithful Generation in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03149v1)
- **Authors**: YiQiu Guo, Yuchen Yang, Zhe Chen, Pingjie Wang, Yusheng Liao, Ya Zhang, Yanfeng Wang, Yu Wang
- **Abstract**: The reliability of large language models remains a critical challenge, particularly due to their susceptibility to hallucinations and factual inaccuracies during text generation. Existing solutions either underutilize models' self-correction with preemptive strategies or use costly post-hoc verification. To further explore the potential of real-time self-verification and correction, we present Dynamic Self-Verify Decoding (DSVD), a novel decoding framework that enhances generation reliability through real-time hallucination detection and efficient error correction. DSVD integrates two key components: (1) parallel self-verification architecture for continuous quality assessment, (2) dynamic rollback mechanism for targeted error recovery. Extensive experiments across five benchmarks demonstrate DSVD's effectiveness, achieving significant improvement in truthfulness (Quesetion-Answering) and factual accuracy (FActScore). Results show the DSVD can be further incorporated with existing faithful decoding methods to achieve stronger performance. Our work establishes that real-time self-verification during generation offers a viable path toward more trustworthy language models without sacrificing practical deployability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "DSVD: Dynamic Self-Verify Decoding for Faithful Generation in Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Dynamic Self-Verify Decoding (DSVD), a novel decoding framework aimed at improving the reliability of large language models (LLMs) by reducing hallucinations and factual inaccuracies during text generation.  DSVD works by incorporating real-time hallucination detection and error correction through two key components: (1) a parallel self-verification architecture that continuously assesses the quality of the generated text, and (2) a dynamic rollback mechanism that allows for targeted error recovery. The framework leverages a semi-supervised hallucination labeling approach to train fine-grained detectors. The experiments demonstrate improvements in truthfulness (TruthfulQA) and factual accuracy (FActScore) on various benchmarks, showing DSVD's effectiveness and its ability to complement existing faithful decoding methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The concept of real-time self-verification during decoding is intriguing. While other methods attempt to improve faithfulness, DSVD's combination of parallel verification and dynamic rollback appears to be a distinct approach.  The semi-supervised approach to training the hallucination detector also represents a novel contribution. Prior works have primarily focused on direct intervention or post-hoc verification. The key innovation is the tight coupling of verification and correction mechanisms during the generation process itself, using internal consistency checks.

* **Significance:** Addressing hallucinations in LLMs is a critical challenge for real-world applications.  The paper shows promising results across different benchmarks and model architectures, suggesting that DSVD has the potential to improve the trustworthiness of LLMs without sacrificing practical deployability (specifically, without adding exorbitant computation costs).  The framework's modular design, demonstrated by its ability to integrate with existing decoding methods, is another significant advantage, as it allows it to be easily incorporated into existing systems. The claimed minimal computational overhead of the method, if thoroughly validated and scalable, is a significant advantage.

* **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly defines the problem and motivates the proposed solution.
    *   The DSVD framework is well-described and the components (hallucination detector, rollback mechanism) are logically explained.
    *   The experimental results are comprehensive, covering multiple models, datasets, and evaluation metrics.
    *   Ablation studies effectively demonstrate the importance of each component in DSVD.
    *   Integration with existing decoding methods showcases the versatility of DSVD.
    *   The latency analysis (Table 5) is particularly important and adds practical value to the findings.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on internal knowledge might limit DSVD's ability to deal with queries requiring up-to-date information (as acknowledged by the authors in the Limitations section). This dependence on internal knowledge could lead to similar biases as those inherent in the LLM itself.
    *   While the paper claims efficiency, a more detailed breakdown of the computational cost compared to other methods (specifically in terms of wall-clock time and resource utilization) would be beneficial.  The 5% extra latency is good, but needs further validation at scale with different hardware profiles.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more thorough qualitative analysis of the errors that DSVD corrects, and the types of errors it still struggles with.
    *   The claim of "negligible latency" increase should be further substantiated with larger and more complex generations, and for smaller model sizes.
    *   The limitations of the semi-supervised approach to hallucination detection training need to be discussed. Are there specific types of hallucinations that are not well detected?
    *  The specific choice of internal states (hidden states from all model layers) for the hallucination detector needs more thorough justification.

* **Impact:** If the claims of efficiency and effectiveness hold true in larger-scale deployments, DSVD could have a significant impact on the field by providing a practical and scalable approach to improving the faithfulness of LLMs. This would make LLMs more reliable in real-world scenarios, especially those where accuracy is paramount.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper demonstrates a reasonably novel and promising approach to a crucial problem in LLMs. The results are compelling, and the modular design is a significant strength. However, the limitations, particularly regarding the dependence on internal knowledge and the need for further validation of computational efficiency, prevent it from receiving a higher score. The need for further theoretical analysis on what kind of latent information is exploited by this approach also diminishes it's strength. The limitations section is also a good addition and shows that the authors recognize the constraints of this work.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### AttackSeqBench: Benchmarking Large Language Models' Understanding of Sequential Patterns in Cyber Attacks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03170v1)
- **Authors**: Javier Yong, Haokai Ma, Yunshan Ma, Anis Yusof, Zhenkai Liang, Ee-Chien Chang
- **Abstract**: The observations documented in Cyber Threat Intelligence (CTI) reports play a critical role in describing adversarial behaviors, providing valuable insights for security practitioners to respond to evolving threats. Recent advancements of Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated significant potential in various cybersecurity applications, including CTI report understanding and attack knowledge graph construction. While previous works have proposed benchmarks that focus on the CTI extraction ability of LLMs, the sequential characteristic of adversarial behaviors within CTI reports remains largely unexplored, which holds considerable significance in developing a comprehensive understanding of how adversaries operate. To address this gap, we introduce AttackSeqBench, a benchmark tailored to systematically evaluate LLMs' capability to understand and reason attack sequences in CTI reports. Our benchmark encompasses three distinct Question Answering (QA) tasks, each task focuses on the varying granularity in adversarial behavior. To alleviate the laborious effort of QA construction, we carefully design an automated dataset construction pipeline to create scalable and well-formulated QA datasets based on real-world CTI reports. To ensure the quality of our dataset, we adopt a hybrid approach of combining human evaluation and systematic evaluation metrics. We conduct extensive experiments and analysis with both fast-thinking and slow-thinking LLMs, while highlighting their strengths and limitations in analyzing the sequential patterns in cyber attacks. The overarching goal of this work is to provide a benchmark that advances LLM-driven CTI report understanding and fosters its application in real-world cybersecurity operations. Our dataset and code are available at https://github.com/Javiery3889/AttackSeqBench .
- **Summary**: Here is a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AttackSeqBench, a benchmark designed to evaluate the capabilities of large language models (LLMs) in understanding and reasoning about sequential attack patterns described in Cyber Threat Intelligence (CTI) reports. The benchmark includes three question-answering (QA) tasks designed to assess LLMs at varying levels of granularity in adversarial behavior (Tactics, Techniques, and Procedures). The authors construct the benchmark dataset automatically from real-world CTI reports, using a pipeline for QA generation and incorporating refinement processes. The benchmark is evaluated with various fast-thinking and slow-thinking LLMs under different settings to highlight the models' strengths and limitations in analyzing sequential cyber attack patterns. The authors provide the dataset and code for the research community.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel and useful direction in cybersecurity-related benchmarks by moving beyond simple extraction to understanding the *sequential* nature of cyberattacks. While previous benchmarks focused on classifying behaviors or extracting information, AttackSeqBench specifically addresses the temporal relationships between adversarial actions as described in CTI reports. This aspect is largely unexplored in existing benchmarks, adding to its value.

*   **Significance:**  The significance lies in the practical relevance of understanding attack sequences for cybersecurity operations.  By benchmarking LLMs on this task, the paper highlights the potential for LLMs to aid in proactive threat detection and incident response. A better understanding of attack progression can help security professionals anticipate future adversarial actions.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Automated Dataset Creation:** The automated dataset construction pipeline is a major strength, allowing for scalability and adaptability to new CTI reports. This is crucial given the evolving nature of cyber threats.
    *   **Focus on Sequential Patterns:**  The core contribution centers around benchmarking LLMs' understanding of the temporal sequence of adversarial behaviors, which is a relevant yet under-explored area.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper tests multiple LLMs under different settings (zero-shot, RAG, regular), offering a comparative analysis.
    *   **Ethics Statement:** The inclusion of the ethics statement, emphasizing the responsible use of CTI reports, and careful consideration of data integrity are important.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity Metric Lacking:** While the study tests for factual recall and coherence with CTI information, it does not include a quantitative comparison to traditional knowledge bases. The potential to expand on this comparison to determine accuracy scores, or metrics on data loss or generalization could potentially strengthen the research.
    *   **LLM Preference:** The authors point out that the results indicate a preference towards answering "yes" on boolean questions. This aspect reveals a limitation of the evaluation metric used, and how it may not accurately assess the LLMs.
    *   **Dataset Refinement Score:** Even though the manual validation score is relatively high, the authors point out that the automatic refinement tool returned lower scores, showing that the automated tool may not perform efficiently, compared to the human validations.

*   **Potential Influence:** The benchmark has the potential to influence the development of more sophisticated LLM-driven cybersecurity tools.  By highlighting the capabilities and limitations of current models, the paper guides future research towards improving LLMs' reasoning about attack sequences. The benchmark could drive advancements in areas like threat prediction and automated response.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** The paper is a valuable contribution, presenting a novel benchmark and a comprehensive evaluation of LLMs for a critical cybersecurity task. The automated dataset creation process and the focus on sequential patterns are notable strengths. The limitations, while not detracting significantly, leave room for further improvement. Future work can further enhance the diversity of questions, develop better automated evaluation metrics, perform quantitative knowledge base comparison, and explore methods to mitigate the LLM preference bias.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Enhancing Cybersecurity in Critical Infrastructure with LLM-Assisted Explainable IoT Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03180v1)
- **Authors**: Ashutosh Ghimire, Ghazal Ghajari, Karma Gurung, Love K. Sah, Fathi Amsaad
- **Abstract**: Ensuring the security of critical infrastructure has become increasingly vital with the proliferation of Internet of Things (IoT) systems. However, the heterogeneous nature of IoT data and the lack of human-comprehensible insights from anomaly detection models remain significant challenges. This paper presents a hybrid framework that combines numerical anomaly detection using Autoencoders with Large Language Models (LLMs) for enhanced preprocessing and interpretability. Two preprocessing approaches are implemented: a traditional method utilizing Principal Component Analysis (PCA) to reduce dimensionality and an LLM-assisted method where GPT-4 dynamically recommends feature selection, transformation, and encoding strategies. Experimental results on the KDDCup99 10% corrected dataset demonstrate that the LLM-assisted preprocessing pipeline significantly improves anomaly detection performance. The macro-average F1 score increased from 0.49 in the traditional PCA-based approach to 0.98 with LLM-driven insights. Additionally, the LLM generates natural language explanations for detected anomalies, providing contextual insights into their causes and implications. This framework highlights the synergy between numerical AI models and LLMs, delivering an accurate, interpretable, and efficient solution for IoT cybersecurity in critical infrastructure.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a hybrid cybersecurity framework for IoT systems that combines autoencoders for numerical anomaly detection with Large Language Models (LLMs) like GPT-4 for improved preprocessing and explainability.  The core idea is to address the limitations of traditional anomaly detection methods, specifically their "black box" nature and reliance on simplistic preprocessing.  The paper compares two preprocessing approaches: a standard PCA-based method and an LLM-assisted method where GPT-4 suggests feature selection, transformation, and encoding strategies. The results demonstrate that the LLM-assisted approach significantly improves anomaly detection performance (using the KDDCup99 dataset) and generates human-readable explanations for detected anomalies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its *integration* of LLMs into both the preprocessing *and* explanation stages of anomaly detection for IoT security. While autoencoders and LLMs have individually been applied to cybersecurity tasks before, this specific combination with LLM-driven preprocessing to directly improve *numerical* anomaly detection accuracy and then provide explanations is a differentiating factor. However, the concept of using LLMs for security log analysis is not entirely new, so the novelty is more in the targeted application to IoT anomaly detection and the specific focus on preprocessing.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance is considerable. IoT systems generate massive amounts of heterogeneous data, making effective and interpretable anomaly detection crucial.  If the proposed framework scales well, it could lead to more accurate and trustworthy cybersecurity solutions for critical infrastructure.  The ability to automatically generate explanations for anomalies would be particularly valuable for security analysts, enabling faster incident response and better understanding of attack vectors. The increased focus on explainability is a move in the right direction for AI based cybersecurity tools.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of IoT security and the limitations of existing anomaly detection methods.
    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** The proposed framework is well-structured and the individual components (autoencoder, LLM integration) are clearly explained.
    *   **Quantifiable Results:** The paper provides quantitative results (F1-score, accuracy) demonstrating the improvement achieved by the LLM-assisted preprocessing approach.
    *   **Explainability Focus:**  The inclusion of LLM-generated explanations addresses a critical gap in many anomaly detection systems.
    *   **Good examples of LLM insight generated**

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dataset limitations:** The KDDCup99 dataset, while a standard benchmark, is old and has known limitations (e.g., artificial traffic patterns, lack of real-world data). This reduces the generalizability of the findings.  Using more realistic and recent IoT datasets would significantly strengthen the paper.
    *   **Limited evaluation of explanations:** While the paper presents example explanations, it lacks a rigorous evaluation of their quality and usefulness. How were these explanations evaluated? Were human subjects involved?  A proper user study or at least a well-defined metric for explanation quality is needed.
    *   **Scalability:** The paper doesn't address the computational cost or scalability of using LLMs for preprocessing, especially for large-scale IoT deployments. LLMs are computationally intensive and can be costly to run.
    *   **Limited ablation analysis:** The authors could perform a more in-depth analysis of the impact of each component of the LLM. How does LLM's feature selection process compare with human-driven approaches, for example?
    *   **Overclaim in abstract:** The abstract states, "This framework highlights the synergy between numerical AI models and LLMs, delivering an accurate, interpretable, and efficient solution..." The paper does not demonstrate an "efficient" solution, as the computational complexity is not measured. This is misleading.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence the development of more interpretable and effective anomaly detection systems for IoT security.  By demonstrating the benefits of LLM integration, it could encourage further research in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a promising approach, but several weaknesses limit its overall impact. The reliance on an outdated dataset and the lack of a rigorous evaluation of the generated explanations are significant shortcomings. While the LLM integration is novel, its practical feasibility and scalability need to be addressed. Given these limitations, a score of 6 is appropriate. It's a solid contribution with potential, but it requires further validation and refinement before its impact can be fully realized.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 6/10

### Structured Outputs Enable General-Purpose LLMs to be Medical Experts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03194v1)
- **Authors**: Guangfu Guo, Kai Zhang, Bryan Hoo, Yujun Cai, Xiaoqian Lu, Nanyun Peng, Yiwei Wang
- **Abstract**: Medical question-answering (QA) is a critical task for evaluating how effectively large language models (LLMs) encode clinical knowledge and assessing their potential applications in medicine. Despite showing promise on multiple-choice tests, LLMs frequently struggle with open-ended medical questions, producing responses with dangerous hallucinations or lacking comprehensive coverage of critical aspects. Existing approaches attempt to address these challenges through domain-specific fine-tuning, but this proves resource-intensive and difficult to scale across models. To improve the comprehensiveness and factuality of medical responses, we propose a novel approach utilizing structured medical reasoning. Our method guides LLMs through an seven-step cognitive process inspired by clinical diagnosis, enabling more accurate and complete answers without additional training. Experiments on the MedLFQA benchmark demonstrate that our approach achieves the highest Factuality Score of 85.8, surpassing fine-tuned models. Notably, this improvement transfers to smaller models, highlighting the method's efficiency and scalability. Our code and datasets are available.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel prompting technique called Medical Structured Output CoT (Med-SoCoT) to improve the factuality and comprehensiveness of medical question-answering (QA) using large language models (LLMs).  Med-SoCoT guides LLMs through a seven-step cognitive process inspired by clinical diagnosis. This structured approach enhances accuracy and completeness without requiring domain-specific fine-tuning.  Experiments on the MedLFQA benchmark demonstrate that Med-SoCoT achieves state-of-the-art factuality scores, even surpassing fine-tuned models.  Notably, the method's effectiveness extends to smaller models, highlighting its efficiency and scalability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its integration of chain-of-thought reasoning with structured outputs, explicitly designed to mimic the cognitive processes of medical professionals. While both CoT and structured output are established techniques, their specific adaptation and implementation within a medical QA framework, particularly without fine-tuning, represents a valuable contribution.  The stepwise reasoning with iterative summarization is also a novel approach to address context length limitations.

*   **Significance:** Medical QA is a challenging and crucial task, and LLMs' limitations in factuality and comprehensiveness pose real risks.  Med-SoCoT's ability to significantly improve factuality scores addresses a critical gap in LLM performance for medical applications.  The fact that it works well even with smaller models is particularly significant as it makes the technology accessible without requiring extensive resources.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Demonstrated improvement in factuality scores on MedLFQA benchmark.
    *   Effective on various LLMs, including smaller models.
    *   Training-free approach, reducing development and maintenance costs.
    *   Clear and well-defined methodology, making it replicable.
    *   Incorporation of clinical reasoning principles from cognitive science.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Relies on a specific structured output format. The effectiveness might be sensitive to prompt design.
    *   Evaluation is limited to the MedLFQA benchmark, so broader applicability needs to be verified.
    *   While the paper reports results on multiple models, further investigation may need to explore model sensitivities or to test different combinations.
    *   The ablation studies are helpful but could be extended to explore different prompt variations in more depth.
    *   While it surpasses fine-tuned model factuality on one benchmark, a direct comparison using other, alternative fine-tuning techniques and datasets would be helpful to demonstrate the absolute advantage in factuality scores.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by:

    *   Providing a practical and efficient method for improving LLMs in medical QA.
    *   Inspiring further research on structured reasoning and prompt engineering in other specialized domains.
    *   Enabling broader adoption of LLMs in healthcare by making them more reliable and accessible.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper's novelty, practical significance, and promising experimental results justify a score of 7. The methodology is well-reasoned and demonstrated effectively, while the benefits of transfer to smaller models are significant. However, the single benchmark limits the generalizability, and a more exhaustive hyperparameter search for the method would improve robustness.
Score: 7

- **Score**: 7/10

### Directly Follows Graphs Go Predictive Process Monitoring With Graph Neural Networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03197v1)
- **Authors**: Attila Lischka, Simon Rauch, Oliver Stritzel
- **Abstract**: In the past years, predictive process monitoring (PPM) techniques based on artificial neural networks have evolved as a method to monitor the future behavior of business processes. Existing approaches mostly focus on interpreting the processes as sequences, so-called traces, and feeding them to neural architectures designed to operate on sequential data such as recurrent neural networks (RNNs) or transformers. In this study, we investigate an alternative way to perform PPM: by transforming each process in its directly-follows-graph (DFG) representation we are able to apply graph neural networks (GNNs) for the prediction tasks. By this, we aim to develop models that are more suitable for complex processes that are long and contain an abundance of loops. In particular, we present different ways to create DFG representations depending on the particular GNN we use. The tested GNNs range from classical node-based to novel edge-based architectures. Further, we investigate the possibility of using multi-graphs. By these steps, we aim to design graph representations that minimize the information loss when transforming traces into graphs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the application of Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) to Predictive Process Monitoring (PPM).  Instead of the common approach of treating process instances (traces) as sequences fed into RNNs or transformers, the authors propose representing traces as Directly-Follows Graphs (DFGs) and leveraging GNNs to make predictions about future process behavior (next activity and remaining time).  The paper presents various DFG construction methods to be used with different GNN architectures (GCN, GAT, and a novel edge-based architecture called GREAT). It investigates both single-edge and multi-edge DFGs. The approach is evaluated on three datasets (Helpdesk, Hospital Billing, and BPIC2012) and benchmarked against LSTM and Transformer baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using GNNs on DFGs for PPM isn't entirely new; the authors themselves cite related work that has explored this space.  However, the paper differentiates itself by:

    *   Exploring a broader range of GNN architectures, including an edge-based one (GREAT), which is somewhat novel in this context.
    *   Systematically investigating different DFG representations and their suitability for various GNNs.
    *   Considering multi-graph DFG representations to minimize information loss during the trace-to-graph transformation.

    The most innovative element appears to be the GREAT architecture paired with edge-based DFG representation. This attempt to create an entirely edge-based representation is a meaningful exploration of how to effectively encode process information within a graph structure, particularly useful for capturing transition dynamics.

*   **Significance:** PPM is an important area with real-world applications in process optimization and anomaly detection. The paper's investigation of GNNs potentially offers benefits over sequence-based models, particularly for complex processes with loops or non-sequential dependencies. The possibility of achieving better generalization and reduced memory requirements with DFG-based GNNs is a significant potential advantage.

    However, the experimental results don't overwhelmingly demonstrate the superiority of the proposed approach.  While the GNNs often perform comparably or slightly better than LSTM/Transformers in next activity prediction, the results are mixed for remaining time prediction. Moreover, the improvements are not always significant, questioning the practical advantage given the higher complexity of GNN implementations. The datasets may not fully represent the kind of complex processes where DFGs and GNNs would really shine. Furthermore, it has been noticed that the reported results for sequence based models might be obtained from less complex models.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper is well-structured and clearly explains the methodology.
    *   The exploration of various DFG representations and GNN architectures is thorough.
    *   It identifies potential advantages of using GNNs for complex processes.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The experimental results aren't strong enough to definitively prove the superiority of GNNs over sequence-based methods.
    *   The datasets used might not be ideal for showcasing the strengths of the proposed approach (complex processes with loops).
    *   The complexity of GNN architectures in comparison to relatively simple sequence-based baselines may outweigh the benefits in practice.
    *   The limited discussion on computational cost and hyperparameter tuning is a weakness, since in reality these represent a significant overhead.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper contributes to the growing body of research on applying graph-based techniques to process mining. While the results are not revolutionary, the paper provides valuable insights into the design choices involved in using GNNs for PPM. It could encourage further research into more sophisticated graph representations and GNN architectures specifically tailored for process data.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the existing literature on applying GNNs to PPM and the somewhat marginal improvements shown in the experiments, the paper's novelty is moderate. While the systematic investigation of DFG representations and the exploration of the GREAT architecture are valuable contributions, the lack of significant performance gains limits the practical impact. More thorough analysis of the effect of computational cost and hyperparameter tuning may be required. More complex processes, or synthetic data generation might better show its benefit.
