### Can Frontier LLMs Replace Annotators in Biomedical Text Mining? Analyzing Challenges and Exploring Solutions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03261v1)
- **Authors**: Yichong Zhao, Susumu Goto
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) can perform various natural language processing (NLP) tasks through in-context learning without relying on supervised data. However, multiple previous studies have reported suboptimal performance of LLMs in biological text mining. By analyzing failure patterns in these evaluations, we identified three primary challenges for LLMs in biomedical corpora: (1) LLMs fail to learn implicit dataset-specific nuances from supervised data, (2) The common formatting requirements of discriminative tasks limit the reasoning capabilities of LLMs particularly for LLMs that lack test-time compute, and (3) LLMs struggle to adhere to annotation guidelines and match exact schemas, which hinders their ability to understand detailed annotation requirements which is essential in biomedical annotation workflow. To address these challenges, we experimented with prompt engineering techniques targeted to the above issues, and developed a pipeline that dynamically extracts instructions from annotation guidelines. Our findings show that frontier LLMs can approach or surpass the performance of state-of-the-art (SOTA) BERT-based models with minimal reliance on manually annotated data and without fine-tuning. Furthermore, we performed model distillation on a closed-source LLM, demonstrating that a BERT model trained exclusively on synthetic data annotated by LLMs can also achieve a practical performance. Based on these results, we explored the feasibility of partially replacing manual annotation with LLMs in production scenarios for biomedical text mining.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Can Frontier LLMs Replace Annotators in Biomedical Text Mining? Analyzing Challenges and Exploring Solutions" investigates the potential of large language models (LLMs) to replace human annotators in biomedical text mining tasks. The authors identify key challenges that LLMs face in this domain, including difficulty capturing dataset-specific nuances, limitations imposed by structured output requirements on reasoning, and struggles to adhere to detailed annotation guidelines.  To address these, they propose prompt engineering techniques, including dynamic few-shot prompting, a two-step inference process, automatic prompt optimization, and dynamically incorporating instructions from annotation guidelines. They show that these techniques allow frontier LLMs to match or exceed the performance of SOTA BERT-based models on some datasets. They also explore model distillation from LLMs to smaller BERT models to improve efficiency. The authors conclude that LLMs can potentially partially replace manual annotation, especially for tasks with less stringent performance requirements.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic analysis of LLM failure modes in biomedical text mining and the targeted prompt engineering strategies developed to overcome these limitations. While individual techniques like dynamic few-shot learning and two-step inference aren't entirely new, their application and adaptation to the specific challenges of biomedical text mining, combined with the dynamic integration of annotation guidelines, demonstrate a valuable contribution. The model distillation from LLM to BERT in this context also adds incremental novelty.

*   **Significance:** The potential to reduce the reliance on manual annotation in biomedical text mining is significant.  Human annotation is a bottleneck, especially with the rapidly expanding volume of biomedical literature. By showing that LLMs can achieve competitive performance with minimal supervision using targeted prompting, the paper offers a pathway toward more efficient knowledge extraction and database curation. The demonstration of distillation to BERT allows for a pragmatic and cost-effective deployment scenario.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper provides a thorough analysis of the reasons behind LLM failures in the biomedical domain.
    *   **Targeted Solutions:** The proposed prompt engineering techniques are directly linked to the identified challenges.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The paper includes experiments on a diverse set of biomedical text mining tasks and datasets.
    *   **Practical Focus:** The exploration of model distillation addresses the practical deployment challenges of LLMs.
    *   Reproducibility: The authors provide the code and prompts, allowing other researchers to reproduce their work.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Automatic Prompt Optimization Success:** The automatic prompt optimization approach yielded only modest gains, suggesting further research is needed.
    *   **Annotation Guidelines' Processing Heuristics**: Rule-based guidelines’ chunking and retrieval for annotation guidelines might be sub-optimal and require further investigation of more advanced techniques.
    *   **Reliance on GPT-4:** The experiments heavily rely on GPT-4. While the results with OpenAI 01-mini are presented, the heavy dependence on a closed-source model limits the generalizability and accessibility of the findings.
    *   **Data Contamination Concerns:** Acknowledging data contamination risks introduces a caveat. While the authors argue for low contamination, it’s an unresolved concern, and future work needs to mitigate it.
    *   Reproducibility: Experiments rely heavily on closed-sourced models (GPT-4) which might not be accessible for all researchers

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings can influence how researchers approach LLM-based biomedical text mining. The proposed prompt engineering strategies provide valuable insights for improving LLM performance in this domain.  The model distillation approach offers a practical solution for deploying LLMs in resource-constrained settings.

**Overall:**

The paper offers a valuable contribution by systematically analyzing LLM limitations in biomedical text mining and developing targeted prompting solutions. The findings offer a pragmatic path towards reducing reliance on human annotation. While limitations exist and the dependence on a closed source GPT-4 is concerning, the paper opens promising avenues for future research.
Score: 7
The score reflects a good contribution that addresses an important problem. A higher score would have been justified if the results were more generalizable (independent of closed-source models), and if a deeper exploration of the prompt optimization was conducted.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### A 262 TOPS Hyperdimensional Photonic AI Accelerator powered by a Si3N4 microcomb laser
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03263v1)
- **Authors**: Christos Pappas, Antonios Prapas, Theodoros Moschos, Manos Kirtas, Odysseas Asimopoulos, Apostolos Tsakyridis, Miltiadis Moralis-Pegios, Chris Vagionas, Nikolaos Passalis, Cagri Ozdilek, Timofey Shpakovsky, Alain Yuji Takabayashi, John D. Jost, Maxim Karpov, Anastasios Tefas, Nikos Pleros
- **Abstract**: The ever-increasing volume of data has necessitated a new computing paradigm, embodied through Artificial Intelligence (AI) and Large Language Models (LLMs). Digital electronic AI computing systems, however, are gradually reaching their physical plateaus, stimulating extensive research towards next-generation AI accelerators. Photonic Neural Networks (PNNs), with their unique ability to capitalize on the interplay of multiple physical dimensions including time, wavelength, and space, have been brought forward with a credible promise for boosting computational power and energy efficiency in AI processors. In this article, we experimentally demonstrate a novel multidimensional arrayed waveguide grating router (AWGR)-based photonic AI accelerator that can execute tensor multiplications at a record-high total computational power of 262 TOPS, offering a ~24x improvement over the existing waveguide-based optical accelerators. It consists of a 16x16 AWGR that exploits the time-, wavelength- and space- division multiplexing (T-WSDM) for weight and input encoding together with an integrated Si3N4-based frequency comb for multi-wavelength generation. The photonic AI accelerator has been experimentally validated in both Fully-Connected (FC) and Convolutional NN (NNs) models, with the FC and CNN being trained for DDoS attack identification and MNIST classification, respectively. The experimental inference at 32 Gbaud achieved a Cohen's kappa score of 0.867 for DDoS detection and an accuracy of 92.14% for MNIST classification, respectively, closely matching the software performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper experimentally demonstrates a novel, integrated photonic AI accelerator for tensor multiplication using a silicon nitride (Si3N4) microcomb laser.  The accelerator uses an arrayed waveguide grating router (AWGR) and time-, wavelength-, and space-division multiplexing (T-W-SDM) to achieve a computational power of 262 TOPS. The authors validate the accelerator's performance with both fully-connected (FC) and convolutional neural networks (CNNs), specifically for DDoS attack identification and MNIST classification.  The experimental results closely match software performance, achieving a Cohen's kappa score of 0.867 for DDoS detection and 92.14% accuracy for MNIST. This is claimed to be a significant improvement over existing waveguide-based optical accelerators.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty of the paper stems from its use of a Si3N4 microcomb laser integrated with an AWGR-based architecture to achieve a high computational throughput. The simultaneous exploitation of time, wavelength, and space division multiplexing is also a key distinguishing factor. While other photonic AI accelerators exist, the reported computational power is claimed to be a significant improvement over current waveguide-based counterparts. However, it is essential to acknowledge that diffractive optics approaches are not directly comparable due to their inherent limitations in flexibility. The paper's novelty score hinges on the claim of substantial improvement over alternative *waveguide-based* designs, which needs closer examination and a precise quantification of the advancements provided.
*   **Significance:** The potential significance lies in addressing the growing computational demands of AI while maintaining energy efficiency. The paper provides experimental evidence that photonic AI accelerators can achieve competitive performance compared to traditional electronic systems, paving the way for future development of peta-scale computing systems. Validation on real-world machine learning tasks further boosts the claims. However, significant challenges remain regarding manufacturability, scalability, cost, and integration complexity for these photonic systems to become viable alternatives to the established electronic infrastructure. The performance is only shown on relatively small problems.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Experimental validation of a complete system.
    *   Demonstration of high computational power for a waveguide-based approach.
    *   Use of standard machine learning tasks (DDoS detection and MNIST classification) for benchmarking.
    *   Detailed description of the architecture and methodology.
    * Optics-informed DL training to adapt to the non-idealities of the optical components
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited benchmarking against other types of photonic architectures (e.g., diffractive).
    *   Potential for high cost and complexity in scaling the design to larger problem sizes (particularly scaling the number of wavelengths and AWGR size).
    *   Lack of a detailed analysis of the energy efficiency compared to electronic systems.
    *   Limited description of specific practical considerations associated with the construction of such systems that might hinder deployment. For example, thermal management of the device is not discussed.
    * The claims of the increase in computational power depend on the baseline for the comparison, which is not justified as clearly as possible.
*   **Impact:** If the demonstrated performance can be sustained while reducing cost and power consumption, this could potentially influence the development of future AI hardware accelerators. It can motivate the development of more efficient designs and integration strategies.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the considerations discussed above, I would rate this paper as a **7**. While the demonstrated computational power is impressive and the integration of a microcomb laser into an AWGR-based architecture for AI acceleration is indeed a significant achievement, several factors limit the overall impact and novelty of the paper: a more thorough comparative performance analysis, lack of a clear argument of the improvements regarding costs and scalability as well as its overall robustness.

### Optimizing for the Shortest Path in Denoising Diffusion Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03265v1)
- **Authors**: Ping Chen, Xingpeng Zhang, Zhaoxiang Liu, Huan Hu, Xiang Liu, Kai Wang, Min Wang, Yanlin Qian, Shiguo Lian
- **Abstract**: In this research, we propose a novel denoising diffusion model based on shortest-path modeling that optimizes residual propagation to enhance both denoising efficiency and quality.Drawing on Denoising Diffusion Implicit Models (DDIM) and insights from graph theory, our model, termed the Shortest Path Diffusion Model (ShortDF), treats the denoising process as a shortest-path problem aimed at minimizing reconstruction error. By optimizing the initial residuals, we improve the efficiency of the reverse diffusion process and the quality of the generated samples.Extensive experiments on multiple standard benchmarks demonstrate that ShortDF significantly reduces diffusion time (or steps) while enhancing the visual fidelity of generated samples compared to prior arts.This work, we suppose, paves the way for interactive diffusion-based applications and establishes a foundation for rapid data generation. Code is available at https://github.com/UnicomAI/ShortDF.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel denoising diffusion model called Shortest Path Diffusion Model (ShortDF).  It leverages graph theory to optimize residual propagation during the denoising process. The core idea is to treat denoising as a shortest-path problem within a graph, aiming to minimize reconstruction errors. The method optimizes the initial residuals to improve the efficiency and quality of generated samples.  Experimental results on standard benchmarks demonstrate that ShortDF reduces diffusion time while improving visual fidelity compared to existing methods. The authors propose this work as a foundation for rapid data generation, particularly useful for interactive applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's central claim of framing the diffusion process as a shortest-path problem is a significant departure from standard diffusion approaches and exhibits strong potential in enhancing efficiency and efficacy. This integration with graph theory to improve the diffusion process demonstrates an original perspective.
*   **Significance:** The paper's primary significance stems from its ability to accelerate the diffusion process without sacrificing image quality. Reducing the number of diffusion steps while maintaining or even improving fidelity has a high practical impact. It is very interesting to see how graph theory could fit in diffusion models for faster data generation.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper provides a clear and concise explanation of the proposed method, ShortDF.
    *   The integration of graph-theoretic concepts is innovative and potentially impactful.
    *   Experimental results on multiple datasets validate the effectiveness of ShortDF in reducing diffusion time and improving image quality. The results consistently outperform or are competitive with state-of-the-art methods, particularly in fewer steps.
    *   The authors provide code to facilitate reproducibility and further research.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the paper clearly describes the method, some aspects of the graph construction and optimization (specifically how network parameters implicitly learn the graph) could be further elaborated upon. A more detailed analysis or visualization of the learned graph structure would strengthen the understanding of the method's inner workings.
    *   The paper lacks a thorough analysis of the computational complexity of the method.
    *   The insights on the performance benefits are mainly grounded on image data. It will be more complete if other data modalities could validate ShortDF's adaptability.

*   **Potential Influence:** If successfully adopted, this approach could significantly impact applications relying on diffusion models, enabling real-time or interactive generation capabilities. The framework's adaptability to broader diffusion applications warrants further investigation.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to accelerate diffusion models using graph theory.  It achieves compelling results, demonstrating a clear advantage in speed and image quality compared to existing methods. Despite the minor limitations regarding clarity in the graph learning mechanism and lack of an in-depth complexity analysis, the paper makes a strong contribution to the field. The idea of optimizing paths in the generation process has great potential.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Conformal Transformations for Symmetric Power Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03269v1)
- **Authors**: Saurabh Kumar, Jacob Buckman, Carles Gelada, Sean Zhang
- **Abstract**: Transformers with linear attention offer significant computational advantages over softmax-based transformers but often suffer from degraded performance. The symmetric power (sympow) transformer, a particular type of linear transformer, addresses some of this performance gap by leveraging symmetric tensor embeddings, achieving comparable performance to softmax transformers. However, the finite capacity of the recurrent state in sympow transformers limits their ability to retain information, leading to performance degradation when scaling the training or evaluation context length. To address this issue, we propose the conformal-sympow transformer, which dynamically frees up capacity using data-dependent multiplicative gating and adaptively stores information using data-dependent rotary embeddings. Preliminary experiments on the LongCrawl64 dataset demonstrate that conformal-sympow overcomes the limitations of sympow transformers, achieving robust performance across scaled training and evaluation contexts.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "conformal-sympow" transformers, an improvement over symmetric power (sympow) transformers, a type of linear attention transformer. Sympow transformers offer computational advantages but suffer performance degradation, especially when scaling context length. Conformal-sympow addresses this limitation by dynamically freeing up capacity in the recurrent state using data-dependent multiplicative gating and adaptively storing information using data-dependent rotary embeddings. These mechanisms form a learned conformal linear transformation, enabling the model to manage limited state capacity more effectively. Experiments on the LongCrawl64 dataset demonstrate that conformal-sympow overcomes the limitations of sympow transformers and achieves robust performance across scaled training and evaluation contexts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper presents two main novel components. First, applying data-dependent gating (from Dao & Gu, 2024) to sympow transformers.  While data-dependent gating isn't new *in general*, its application to sympow transformers is a novel combination.  Second, and more significantly, the paper introduces a *data-dependent* approach to learning the rotation rates of rotary embeddings in the *sympow* context.  The idea of scaling rotation rates based on the input data is innovative and distinguishes this work. The combination of both gating and learned rotations to create a "conformal" transformation is also a novel contribution.

* **Significance:** The significance lies in addressing a practical problem of linear attention transformers: their limited capacity to handle long contexts effectively. Sympow transformers are promising, but this paper enhances their practicality for real-world scenarios. The improvements detailed appear to give the *sympow* transformer better scaling behavior when training on larger contexts and when evaluating on contexts longer than seen during training. This is a valuable contribution as it extends the applicability of a computationally efficient architecture.  The paper's experiments on the LongCrawl64 dataset serve as concrete validation of the proposed approach. By demonstrating that conformal-sympow maintains or improves performance while mitigating degradation with increased context length, the authors present convincing evidence of the architecture's benefits. The theoretical grounding in conformal transformations, while not entirely fundamental, provides a solid framework for understanding the introduced mechanisms. However, the use of the attention formulation of sympow introduces a limitation, as it is computationally more expensive. This approach, although valid for research, is not what would be preferred for deployment and efficient scaling, thus weakening the real-world significance somewhat.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the scaling limitations of sympow transformers.
    * **Well-Defined Solution:** The proposed conformal-sympow architecture is well-motivated and explained.
    * **Empirical Validation:** Experiments on a standard long-context benchmark provide solid evidence of the method's effectiveness.
    * **Theoretical Justification:** The connection to conformal transformations provides a strong foundation.
    * The paper is clearly written and well-organized, facilitating understanding.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Improvement:** While the combination is novel, the gating mechanism is borrowed from prior work. This does reduce the perceived impact.
    * **Computational Considerations:** The experimental validation relied on the computationally expensive attention formulation and therefore may not directly translate to the efficient, chunked, recurrent formulation used in deployment.
    * **Limited Exploration:** The authors acknowledge that the space of gating strategies and learned rotation approaches is vast and they only explore one specific instantiation.
    * Lack of a broader range of tasks is limiting; the evaluation only uses LongCrawl64.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research on linear attention mechanisms by highlighting the importance of adaptive state management and inspiring novel approaches for learning positional embeddings in this context. It provides concrete methods to better take advantage of the benefits of linear-time architectures while minimizing the performance penalties.

**Justification of Score:**

The conformal-sympow transformer offers a useful and well-validated improvement to sympow transformers, addressing a key limitation regarding context length scalability. However, some aspects of this paper represent more of an integration of existing concepts than groundbreaking developments. The dependence on the attention formulation, while providing solid experiments, weakens the significance when considering actual deployment. Therefore, this paper is a solid, incremental contribution but isn't quite at the level of a fundamentally transformative advancement.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### SEOE: A Scalable and Reliable Semantic Evaluation Framework for Open Domain Event Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03303v1)
- **Authors**: Yi-Fan Lu, Xian-Ling Mao, Tian Lan, Tong Zhang, Yu-Shi Zhu, Heyan Huang
- **Abstract**: Automatic evaluation for Open Domain Event Detection (ODED) is a highly challenging task, because ODED is characterized by a vast diversity of un-constrained output labels from various domains. Nearly all existing evaluation methods for ODED usually first construct evaluation benchmarks with limited labels and domain coverage, and then evaluate ODED methods using metrics based on token-level label matching rules. However, this kind of evaluation framework faces two issues: (1) The limited evaluation benchmarks lack representatives of the real world, making it difficult to accurately reflect the performance of various ODED methods in real-world scenarios; (2) Evaluation metrics based on token-level matching rules fail to capture semantic similarity between predictions and golden labels. To address these two problems above, we propose a scalable and reliable Semantic-level Evaluation framework for Open domain Event detection (SEOE) by constructing a more representative evaluation benchmark and introducing a semantic evaluation metric. Specifically, our proposed framework first constructs a scalable evaluation benchmark that currently includes 564 event types covering 7 major domains, with a cost-effective supplementary annotation strategy to ensure the benchmark's representativeness. The strategy also allows for the supplement of new event types and domains in the future. Then, the proposed SEOE leverages large language models (LLMs) as automatic evaluation agents to compute a semantic F1-score, incorporating fine-grained definitions of semantically similar labels to enhance the reliability of the evaluation. Extensive experiments validate the representatives of the benchmark and the reliability of the semantic evaluation metric. Existing ODED methods are thoroughly evaluated, and the error patterns of predictions are analyzed, revealing several insightful findings.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper, focusing on rigor and justification for the assigned score:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SEOE, a Semantic-level Evaluation framework for Open Domain Event Detection (ODED). It addresses limitations of existing ODED evaluation methods, which rely on limited-label benchmarks and token-level matching, by constructing a larger, more representative benchmark and utilizing large language models (LLMs) for semantic evaluation. SEOE integrates existing event extraction datasets, employs a cost-effective annotation strategy to ensure comprehensive annotation coverage, and leverages LLMs to compute semantic F1-scores. Experimental results validate the benchmark's representativeness and the metric's reliability, providing a more comprehensive and nuanced evaluation of ODED models. The benchmark and evaluation toolkit are publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses a significant problem:** ODED evaluation is notoriously challenging due to the open-ended nature of the task. SEOE directly tackles the shortcomings of existing evaluation methods, providing a valuable contribution to the field.
    *   **Scalable and representative benchmark:** The construction of a new benchmark covering multiple domains and utilizing LLMs for annotation has significantly increased dataset size and diversity, enhancing real-world scenario representation.
    *   **Semantic-level evaluation metric:** Moving beyond token-level matching by using LLMs to capture semantic similarity between predicted and golden labels is a clear improvement. This addresses the issue of accurately evaluating semantically similar but lexically different labels. The group-based method is a smart way to inject ontological reasoning.
    *   **Thorough evaluation:** The experiments are comprehensive, evaluating a wide range of existing ODED methods (including closed- and open-source LLMs) and analyzing error patterns.
    *   **Reproducibility:** Public availability of the benchmark and evaluation toolkits is a major plus, promoting future research and comparison.
    * **Nucleus Sampling enhancement of dataset represents a thoughtful approach to improving evaluation robustness.**

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **LLM reliance:** While LLMs provide a more sophisticated evaluation, they are not perfect. The subjective nature of LLM-based judgements and the potential for biases are legitimate concerns. The paper addresses this to some extent through human evaluation comparison, but further analysis of specific failure cases and biases in the LLM evaluation would strengthen the work.
    *   **Evaluation Reliance on specific LLMs:** The SEOE framework relies heavily on specific versions of GPT. Future iterations or other LLMs may produce different results or be superior which may alter the current findings.
    *   **Incomplete discussion of limitations:** The limitations section is somewhat cursory. More detail on specific edge cases or challenges in annotation or evaluation would be beneficial.
    *   **Lack of novel technical contribution beyond framework construction**: The core contribution centers around the construction of the evaluation framework and benchmark. Although valuable, the paper lacks a novel algorithmic contribution for solving the ODED task itself.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   **Significant improvement to Evaluation Methodology:** The shift from token-level to semantic-level evaluation is a major step forward. The approach aligns with the increasing sophistication of ODED models and the need for evaluation metrics that reflect actual performance in real-world scenarios.
    *   **Benchmark size and Domain Coverage:** The benchmark has significantly increased the quantity and diversity of event extraction datasets for the ODED space.
    *   **Community Resource:** The public availability of the resources will encourage future investigations and act as a good benchmark for ODED development.

**Justification for Score:**

The SEOE framework addresses a crucial need for more reliable and representative evaluation of ODED methods. The combination of a large, diverse benchmark and a semantic-level evaluation metric based on LLMs is a significant improvement over existing approaches.  The public availability further increases the impact. However, the reliance on potentially biased LLMs for evaluation and the lack of a novel technical contribution beyond benchmark creation prevent a higher score.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLM as GNN: Graph Vocabulary Learning for Text-Attributed Graph Foundation Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03313v1)
- **Authors**: Xi Zhu, Haochen Xue, Ziwei Zhao, Wujiang Xu, Jingyuan Huang, Minghao Guo, Qifan Wang, Kaixiong Zhou, Yongfeng Zhang
- **Abstract**: Text-Attributed Graphs (TAGs), where each node is associated with text descriptions, are ubiquitous in real-world scenarios. They typically exhibit distinctive structure and domain-specific knowledge, motivating the development of a Graph Foundation Model (GFM) that generalizes across diverse graphs and tasks. Despite large efforts to integrate Large Language Models (LLMs) and Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) for TAGs, existing approaches suffer from decoupled architectures with two-stage alignment, limiting their synergistic potential. Even worse, existing methods assign out-of-vocabulary (OOV) tokens to graph nodes, leading to graph-specific semantics, token explosion, and incompatibility with task-oriented prompt templates, which hinders cross-graph and cross-task transferability. To address these challenges, we propose PromptGFM, a versatile GFM for TAGs grounded in graph vocabulary learning. PromptGFM comprises two key components: (1) Graph Understanding Module, which explicitly prompts LLMs to replicate the finest GNN workflow within the text space, facilitating seamless GNN-LLM integration and elegant graph-text alignment; (2) Graph Inference Module, which establishes a language-based graph vocabulary ensuring expressiveness, transferability, and scalability, enabling readable instructions for LLM fine-tuning. Extensive experiments demonstrate our superiority and transferability across diverse graphs and tasks. The code is available at this: https://github.com/agiresearch/PromptGFM.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary, critical evaluation, and novelty assessment of the paper "LLM as GNN: Graph Vocabulary Learning for Text-Attributed Graph Foundation Models."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PromptGFM, a Graph Foundation Model (GFM) for Text-Attributed Graphs (TAGs) that aims to address limitations in existing approaches that integrate Large Language Models (LLMs) and Graph Neural Networks (GNNs). The core idea is to learn a graph vocabulary within the text space, enabling seamless GNN-LLM integration and improved transferability across diverse graphs and tasks. PromptGFM consists of two main components:

1.  **Graph Understanding Module:** This module prompts LLMs to explicitly replicate the GNN workflow within the text space, achieving fine-grained alignment between LLMs and GNNs. Essentially, it's turning the LLM into a GNN by prompting it to perform GNN operations.

2.  **Graph Inference Module:** This module establishes a language-based graph vocabulary by mapping nodes to a finite sequence of language-based tokens. This addresses the out-of-vocabulary (OOV) token problem and improves transferability. The resulting language-based IDs are then used in prompts for fine-tuning LLMs.

The paper claims superior performance and transferability compared to existing methods, demonstrated through extensive experiments across various graphs and tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses an important problem:** The paper targets a key challenge in graph representation learning: building GFMs that generalize across diverse datasets and tasks, particularly for TAGs.  The OOV token problem and the lack of tight integration between GNNs and LLMs are well-identified limitations.
    *   **Novel Approach:**  The idea of explicitly prompting LLMs to perform GNN operations *within the text space* is a creative and potentially impactful approach. This sidesteps the embedding space alignment problems encountered by many other methods. The language-based graph vocabulary is also a promising approach to overcome OOV issues and enable transfer learning.
    *   **Sound Methodology:** The paper describes the PromptGFM framework in sufficient detail, and the experimental setup appears well-designed, comparing against a reasonable set of baselines and across a variety of datasets.  The ablation studies help to demonstrate the importance of each module.
    *   **Good Empirical Results:**  The experimental results generally support the paper's claims of improved performance and transferability. The reported gains over existing methods seem significant.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** The framework involves multiple steps (prompt engineering, GNN replication in text, graph vocabulary learning, LLM fine-tuning), which may make it challenging to implement and optimize in practice. The reliance on prompting and large language models also brings the typical limitations of LLMs (computational cost, prompt sensitivity).
    *   **Reliance on Prompting:** While prompting LLMs to act like GNNs is novel, the stability and robustness of this approach are always a concern. LLMs can be sensitive to prompt variations, and the effectiveness of the approach depends heavily on the quality of the prompts.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Justification:**  The paper provides a solid intuition for why the approach should work, but lacks a strong theoretical analysis. Some formalization of why the language-based vocabulary leads to better transferability could strengthen the paper.
    *   **Limited Discussion of Limitations:** Although the authors acknowledge limitations, more discussion of potential failure cases or scenarios where PromptGFM might underperform would be beneficial. The negative transfer observed in inter-domain experiments needs a deeper analysis.
    *   **Concerns about reproducibility:** The paper heavily relies on the OpenAI's GPT-4 model, which is not open source and may have changing behavior.
*   **Novelty and Significance:**

The novelty lies in the combination of several ideas in a unique way: 1) Viewing an LLM as a GNN, 2) Performing GNN operations within the text space through careful prompting, and 3) Learning language-based IDs. These ideas together attempt to solve some key practical challenges of applying LLMs to graph structured data. If the approach proves robust and generalizable, it could have a significant impact on how we integrate LLMs and GNNs for various applications involving TAGs. However, more careful and rigorous study is needed to assess if the method will be used in practice.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and well-motivated approach to a challenging problem. The empirical results are promising, and the ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each module. However, the reliance on prompting, the potential for complexity in implementation, the lack of theoretical justification, and OpenAI API reliance are important limitations that prevent it from achieving a higher score. While the idea is original and potentially impactful, the success of PromptGFM critically hinges on the prompt engineering and stability of underlying LLMs, which make it slightly risky.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### EnigmaToM: Improve LLMs' Theory-of-Mind Reasoning Capabilities with Neural Knowledge Base of Entity States
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03340v1)
- **Authors**: Hainiu Xu, Siya Qi, Jiazheng Li, Yuxiang Zhou, Jinhua Du, Caroline Catmur, Yulan He
- **Abstract**: Theory-of-Mind (ToM), the ability to infer others' perceptions and mental states, is fundamental to human interaction but remains a challenging task for Large Language Models (LLMs). While existing ToM reasoning methods show promise with reasoning via perceptual perspective-taking, they often rely excessively on LLMs, reducing their efficiency and limiting their applicability to high-order ToM reasoning, which requires multi-hop reasoning about characters' beliefs. To address these issues, we present EnigmaToM, a novel neuro-symbolic framework that enhances ToM reasoning by integrating a Neural Knowledge Base of entity states (Enigma) for (1) a psychology-inspired iterative masking mechanism that facilitates accurate perspective-taking and (2) knowledge injection that elicits key entity information. Enigma generates structured representations of entity states, which construct spatial scene graphs -- leveraging spatial information as an inductive bias -- for belief tracking of various ToM orders and enhancing events with fine-grained entity state details. Experimental results on multiple benchmarks, including ToMi, HiToM, and FANToM, show that EnigmaToM significantly improves ToM reasoning across LLMs of varying sizes, particularly excelling in high-order reasoning scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "EnigmaToM: Improve LLMs' Theory-of-Mind Reasoning Capabilities with Neural Knowledge Base of Entity States":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EnigmaToM, a neuro-symbolic framework designed to enhance the Theory-of-Mind (ToM) reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). EnigmaToM leverages a neural knowledge base (Enigma) containing entity states, coupled with an iterative masking mechanism for perceptual perspective-taking and knowledge injection to provide fine-grained entity state details to the LLM. By creating structured representations of entity states in spatial scene graphs, EnigmaToM aims to improve LLMs' belief tracking abilities, particularly in higher-order ToM reasoning scenarios.  Experiments on benchmarks like ToMi, HiToM, and FANTOM suggest that EnigmaToM improves performance across LLMs of varying sizes, especially in multi-hop reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates decent novelty through the integrated design of a neuro-symbolic system for ToM. Key novel components include:

    *   Neural Knowledge Base (Enigma): Utilizes OpenPI2.0 as a knowledge source to represent entity states within events.
    *   Iterative Masking (IM): A psychology-inspired iterative masking mechanism to facilitate perceptual perspective-taking.
    *   Knowledge Injection (KI): Knowledge injection mechanism to provide fine-grained entity state information, enhancing the LLM's understanding of events.

    While some individual components (knowledge injection, neuro-symbolic approaches) aren't entirely new, the way they are combined and applied specifically to address the limitations of LLMs in high-order ToM reasoning presents a unique contribution. The iterative masking mechanism, grounded in psychological theories, appears particularly innovative.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from the observed improvements in LLMs' ToM reasoning capabilities, especially in higher-order scenarios. The ability of EnigmaToM to enhance LLMs' performance by incorporating knowledge from OpenPI2.0, thereby reducing the demand on the reasoning capability of LLMs is a significant contribution.

    However, some points need consideration:

    *   *Benchmark Selection:* While ToMi, HiToM, and FANTOM are standard datasets, their limitations should be acknowledged. The reliance on these benchmarks makes it challenging to assess the real-world applicability of the improvements.

    *   *Ablation and Analysis:* The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of individual components (IM and KI).  However, a more detailed analysis of the types of errors reduced or introduced by EnigmaToM would further strengthen the paper. For instance, the study needs to analyze which type of causal and spatial relationships contribute the most.

    *   *Comparison with Existing Neuro-Symbolic Approaches:* The comparison with symbolic approaches (e.g. SymbolicToM) is useful, but a more in-depth comparison highlighting the efficiency gains and qualitative differences in reasoning would be valuable. The analysis of limitations of SymbolicToM and demonstration on scalability could be further improved.

    *   *Hallucination:* The results for FANTOM when scaling the NKB seem to increase hallucination. This should be addressed.

*   **Strengths:**
    * Addresses a crucial limitation of LLMs - ToM reasoning, especially in high-order scenarios.
    * Presents a novel neuro-symbolic architecture with clear separation of components.
    * Demonstrates performance improvements across various LLMs and datasets.
    * Provides ablation studies to demonstrate the contribution of individual components.
    * The framework is built on well-established psychological theories.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    * Relies on existing ToM benchmarks with potential limitations regarding real-world applicability.
    * More detailed error analysis and comparison with existing neuro-symbolic approaches would be beneficial.
    * Addressing the hallucination issue in the FANTOM dataset would make the paper more robust.
    * The significance of the results need more analysis.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a significant contribution to the field of ToM reasoning in LLMs by proposing an original and well-structured neuro-symbolic framework. The iterative masking mechanism and knowledge injection techniques demonstrate improvements over existing methods, particularly in complex, high-order reasoning tasks. The ablation studies are important to highlight the individual effects of the mechanisms.

However, the paper isn't without limitations. The reliance on existing benchmarks and need for further analysis of generated errors restricts the impact. Moreover, a detailed comparison of the scaling effect with prior neuro-symbolic approaches could strengthen the paper. Addressing the weaknesses would significantly enhance the paper's overall novelty, significance, and impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Leveraging Large Language Models to Develop Heuristics for Emerging Optimization Problems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03350v1)
- **Authors**: Thomas Bömer, Nico Koltermann, Max Disselnmeyer, Laura Dörr, Anne Meyer
- **Abstract**: Combinatorial optimization problems often rely on heuristic algorithms to generate efficient solutions. However, the manual design of heuristics is resource-intensive and constrained by the designer's expertise. Recent advances in artificial intelligence, particularly large language models (LLMs), have demonstrated the potential to automate heuristic generation through evolutionary frameworks. Recent works focus only on well-known combinatorial optimization problems like the traveling salesman problem and online bin packing problem when designing constructive heuristics. This study investigates whether LLMs can effectively generate heuristics for niche, not yet broadly researched optimization problems, using the unit-load pre-marshalling problem as an example case. We propose the Contextual Evolution of Heuristics (CEoH) framework, an extension of the Evolution of Heuristics (EoH) framework, which incorporates problem-specific descriptions to enhance in-context learning during heuristic generation. Through computational experiments, we evaluate CEoH and EoH and compare the results. Results indicate that CEoH enables smaller LLMs to generate high-quality heuristics more consistently and even outperform larger models. Larger models demonstrate robust performance with or without contextualized prompts. The generated heuristics exhibit scalability to diverse instance configurations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) for automating the generation of heuristics for a niche combinatorial optimization problem called the Unit-load Pre-marshalling Problem (UPMP). The authors introduce the Contextual Evolution of Heuristics (CEoH) framework, an extension of the existing Evolution of Heuristics (EoH) framework.  CEoH enhances heuristic generation by incorporating problem-specific descriptions into the prompts given to LLMs, aiming to improve in-context learning. The paper compares CEoH and EoH using several LLMs, both open-source and closed-source, and evaluates the performance of the generated heuristics on UPMP instances with varying configurations. The results suggest that CEoH can enable smaller LLMs to generate higher-quality heuristics more consistently, while larger models demonstrate robust performance with or without the contextualized prompts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in:
    *   **Application to a Niche Problem:** Extending LLM-based heuristic generation to a less-studied, practically relevant optimization problem (UPMP). Prior work focused largely on TSP or Bin Packing. This addresses a significant gap.
    *   **CEoH Framework:** Introduction of the CEoH framework, an extension of EoH, providing a specific mechanism (contextual prompts) to improve LLM's understanding of the problem constraints during heuristic generation.
    *   **Comparative Study:**  A rigorous comparison of CEoH and EoH across various LLMs, highlighting the impact of contextual information on different model architectures and sizes.

* **Significance:**
    *   **Practical Implications:** The work contributes to automating the development of efficient heuristics for combinatorial optimization, a process typically reliant on human expertise and time.  The UPMP, while niche, has real-world applications in warehouse management.
    *   **Insights into LLM Capabilities:** The paper offers insights into how LLMs can be effectively used for heuristic design, particularly how contextual information influences the quality of generated heuristics.  The findings that smaller, specialized models can outperform larger general models under specific conditions are valuable.
    *   **Framework for Future Research:** The CEoH framework provides a template for future research exploring LLM-based heuristic design in other niche optimization domains.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-Defined Problem:** The UPMP is clearly defined, enabling a focused evaluation.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The CEoH framework is described in detail, allowing for reproducibility.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experimental setup is thorough, considering various LLMs, prompt strategies, and instance configurations. The use of the lower bound as a benchmark is a useful practice.
    *   **Analysis of Results:** The paper provides a thoughtful discussion of the results, including possible explanations for observed behaviors (e.g., the potential for over-constraining larger models with CEoH).

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Problem Scope:** While focusing on a niche problem is a strength in some respects, it also limits the generalizability of the findings.  The applicability of CEoH to significantly different optimization problems needs further investigation.
    *   **Dependency on LLM Quality:**  The performance of the framework is inherently dependent on the capabilities of the underlying LLM. The study doesn't delve deeply into the influence of specific LLM training data or architectures.
    *   **Runtime of the A* Algorithm:** Performance is only compared against one other approach for finding a lower bound and for optimality comparison.
    *   **Fitness Function:** The fitness function employed is relatively simple. The paper could discuss alternative fitness functions and potential improvements.
    *   **Lack of Theoretical Analysis:**  The paper is primarily empirical. A more theoretical analysis of why CEoH works (or doesn't work) for certain models could strengthen the conclusions.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in automated algorithm design and the application of LLMs to optimization. By demonstrating the effectiveness of contextual information, it encourages further exploration of prompt engineering techniques. The CEoH framework could be adopted and adapted for other optimization problems.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by exploring a relatively unexplored area and providing a concrete framework and empirical evidence. While the findings are specific to the UPMP, the insights gained are transferable to other similar optimization problems. The identified weaknesses suggest avenues for future research, rather than undermining the current contribution. Considering the novelty of the CEoH framework and its application to the UPMP, along with a fairly comprehensive experiment, I assign a score that reflects the significance of this contribution.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Video Super-Resolution: All You Need is a Video Diffusion Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03355v1)
- **Authors**: Zhihao Zhan, Wang Pang, Xiang Zhu, Yechao Bai
- **Abstract**: We present a generic video super-resolution algorithm in this paper, based on the Diffusion Posterior Sampling framework with an unconditional video generation model in latent space. The video generation model, a diffusion transformer, functions as a space-time model. We argue that a powerful model, which learns the physics of the real world, can easily handle various kinds of motion patterns as prior knowledge, thus eliminating the need for explicit estimation of optical flows or motion parameters for pixel alignment. Furthermore, a single instance of the proposed video diffusion transformer model can adapt to different sampling conditions without re-training. Due to limited computational resources and training data, our experiments provide empirical evidence of the algorithm's strong super-resolution capabilities using synthetic data.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel video super-resolution (VSR) algorithm called VDM-VSR, which leverages Diffusion Posterior Sampling (DPS) with an unconditional video diffusion model (VDM) in the latent space.  The core idea is to use a DiT-based VDM (essentially a space-time model) as a powerful prior that implicitly learns motion patterns from training data, thereby eliminating the need for explicit motion estimation (e.g., optical flow). The algorithm formulates VSR as an inverse problem, combining likelihood estimation from low-resolution (LR) observations with the prior probability estimation from the VDM. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of their approach using a synthetic Moving MNIST dataset, showing that inter-frame information improves super-resolution results and that the algorithm is robust to varying levels of aliasing in the LR inputs.  The authors' approach avoids explicit motion estimation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novelty in Approach:** The paper explores a relatively underexplored direction in VSR: using VDMs as a learned prior to implicitly handle motion, bypassing traditional motion estimation methods.  This is a significant departure from most existing VSR techniques.
    *   **Sound Theoretical Foundation:** The algorithm is based on the well-established DPS framework, providing a solid theoretical basis.
    *   **Potential for Generalization:** The use of an *unconditional* VDM is a key strength.  In theory, it should allow the model to be applied to different sensing conditions or degradation models without retraining, a significant advantage over conditional diffusion models.
    *   **Interesting Experimental Results:** The experiments, albeit on synthetic data, provide initial evidence that the approach works. The analysis of the influence of frame order and aliasing is insightful.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Experimental Validation:** The primary weakness is the use of the synthetic Moving MNIST dataset for evaluation.  This dataset is *far* from representative of real-world video data and limits the conclusions that can be drawn. The paper explicitly acknowledges this limitation, citing limited computational resources.  The results don't really generalize.
    *   **Lack of Comparison to State-of-the-Art:** The paper lacks a quantitative comparison with existing state-of-the-art VSR algorithms.  Without such a comparison, it's impossible to assess the actual performance of VDM-VSR relative to competing methods.
    *   **Computational Cost Concerns:** VDMs, particularly those based on transformers, are notoriously computationally expensive. The authors mention the need for large-scale models, which would exacerbate this problem. The paper doesn't provide any details about the computational complexity of the algorithm.
    *   **Unconditional DM limitations:** The assumption that an *unconditional* diffusion model can inherently learn and model all motion patterns sufficiently well for VSR is a strong one. In more complex real-world scenes with many different objects moving independently, this assumption might not hold.
    *   **VAE Dependency:** The method relies on a VAE for dimensionality reduction. The quality of the reconstructed high-resolution images is therefore also affected by the quality of the VAE.

*   **Significance and Potential Impact:**

    The *idea* of using VDMs as a prior for implicit motion handling in VSR is significant. If the algorithm could be scaled to real-world datasets and demonstrated competitive performance, it would represent a major advance. The potential to avoid explicit motion estimation could lead to more robust and efficient VSR algorithms. This aligns with the trend in the field to leverage generative models. However, at its current stage, its real-world impact is limited.

*   **Overall Assessment:**

    The paper presents an intriguing and potentially significant idea, but the lack of experimental validation on real-world data and a quantitative comparison to existing methods significantly weakens the claims. The reliance on synthetic data only demonstrates feasibility, not actual performance or superiority. It's a good *proof of concept* but a long way from being a practical solution. The discussion of the method is solid but does not provide enough information on its computational costs. The limitations in the paper are clearly stated which is a positive.

**Score: 5**

**Justification:**

A score of 5 reflects the balance between the novelty of the idea and the limitations in the experimental validation. The idea of using unconditional VDMs for motion-aware VSR is novel and potentially impactful (3 points). The well-defined theoretical foundation and the insightful analysis of synthetic data provide some support for the approach (1 point). However, the lack of real-world experiments and comparison to state-of-the-art methods significantly diminishes the paper's overall significance (negative points that remove two points, the lack of evaluation is too strong). The computational cost is not discussed which is a further issue. As such, the score is 5. While the *concept* is promising, the *contribution* in its current form is modest. It serves primarily as a starting point for further research.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 5/10

### Transformers for molecular property prediction: Domain adaptation efficiently improves performance
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03360v1)
- **Authors**: Afnan Sultan, Max Rausch-Dupont, Shahrukh Khan, Olga Kalinina, Andrea Volkamer, Dietrich Klakow
- **Abstract**: Most of the current transformer-based chemical language models are pre-trained on millions to billions of molecules. However, the improvement from such scaling in dataset size is not confidently linked to improved molecular property prediction. The aim of this study is to investigate and overcome some of the limitations of transformer models in predicting molecular properties. Specifically, we examine the impact of pre-training dataset size and diversity on the performance of transformer models and investigate the use of domain adaptation as a technique for improving model performance. First, our findings indicate that increasing pretraining dataset size beyond 400K molecules from the GuacaMol dataset does not result in a significant improvement on four ADME endpoints, namely, solubility, permeability, microsomal stability, and plasma protein binding. Second, our results demonstrate that using domain adaptation by further training the transformer model on a small set of domain-relevant molecules, i.e., a few hundred to a few thousand, using multi-task regression of physicochemical properties was sufficient to significantly improve performance for three out of the four investigated ADME endpoints (P-value < 0.001). Finally, we observe that a model pre-trained on 400K molecules and domain adopted on a few hundred/thousand molecules performs similarly (P-value > 0.05) to more complicated transformer models like MolBERT(pre-trained on 1.3M molecules) and MolFormer (pre-trained on 100M molecules). A comparison to a random forest model trained on basic physicochemical properties showed similar performance to the examined transformer models. We believe that current transformer models can be improved through further systematic analysis of pre-training and downstream data, pre-training objectives, and scaling laws, ultimately leading to better and more helpful models.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "Transformers for Molecular Property Prediction: Domain Adaptation Efficiently Improves Performance."

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the impact of pre-training dataset size and domain adaptation on the performance of transformer models for molecular property prediction (MPP), specifically for ADME (Absorption, Distribution, Metabolism, Excretion) endpoints. The authors find that simply scaling the pre-training dataset size beyond a certain point (~400K molecules) does not significantly improve performance. They attribute this to a lack of diversity (specifically, low diversity of Murcko scaffolds) in larger pre-training datasets compared to the downstream datasets.  The key finding is that domain adaptation, achieved by further training the model on a smaller, domain-relevant dataset using multi-task regression (MTR) of physicochemical properties, significantly improves prediction accuracy for solubility, permeability, and microsomal stability. The resulting models show performance comparable to more complex transformer architectures like MolBERT and MolFormer, even when the simpler models are pre-trained on significantly less data, and have a similar performance to a random forest model. The authors conclude that the transformer models in chemistry are currently limited due to redundancy in the pre-training dataset and are not tailored for the specific needs and nuances of molecular data and that current models can be improved through further systematic analysis on pre-training and downstream data, pre-training objectives, and scaling laws.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   *Clear Research Question:* The paper addresses a relevant and important question in the application of transformers to chemistry: does simply scaling up pre-training data always lead to better downstream performance?
    *   *Well-Defined Experiments:* The experimental setup is well-defined, with careful control of dataset sizes, training objectives, and evaluation metrics.
    *   *Meaningful Findings:* The finding that domain adaptation is more effective than simply increasing pre-training data size is significant.  It challenges the prevailing trend of solely focusing on large-scale pre-training in the field.
    *   *Practical Implications:* The work has practical implications for researchers working on MPP. It suggests a more efficient strategy: focus on targeted domain adaptation with simpler models, rather than blindly increasing pre-training dataset size.
    *   *Comparison with Baselines:* The comparison with established models like MolBERT and MolFormer, and the random forest baseline, provides a strong benchmark for evaluating the proposed approach.
    *   *Thorough Analysis:* The authors delve into the reasons behind their findings, including diversity of data (Murcko scaffold analysis) and the impact of different training objectives, which strengthens the paper.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   *Limited Scope of ADME Endpoints:* The study focuses primarily on four ADME endpoints. While these are important, a broader investigation across more diverse property types would further strengthen the conclusions. The plasma protein binding datasets had a small sample size, weakening the conclusions drawn from those datasets.
    *   *Lack of Novel Transformer Architecture:* The paper uses a standard BERT-like architecture. While this is justified by the focus on pre-training and domain adaptation strategies, exploring novel transformer architectures tailored for molecular data could have further enhanced the contribution.
    *   *Limited exploration of data augmentation during Domain Adaptation:* The authors experimented with Contrastive Learning, but did not fully test its efficacy in the given setting. The authors could have also explored other methods like random SMILES augmentation.
    *   *Random Forest setup is questionable:* It is not explicitly clear in the paper why the chosen Random Forest approach was taken. It is trained on physicochemcial properties, which are already calculated with RDKit. The Random Forest model may have shown similar performance to the transformer architecture, but not with an apples-to-apples comparison.
    *   *Lack of a scaling law:* the authors should have performed a simple scaling law for the transformer model.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The *negative result* regarding the simple scaling of pre-training data is valuable, as it pushes the field to think beyond just "bigger is better."
    *   The emphasis on domain adaptation using multi-task regression and the analysis of data diversity are significant contributions. The paper suggests a more nuanced and efficient approach to building effective MPP models.
    *   The comparison to more complex transformer models, demonstrating comparable performance with simpler models and less data, is impactful.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper provides meaningful insights into the training and application of transformer models for molecular property prediction. The practical implications of efficient domain adaptation are valuable, and the analysis is generally well-supported by experimental evidence.

The identified weaknesses, such as the limited scope of ADME endpoints, and the lack of a novel architecture, and questioning the Random Forest setup, prevent the paper from achieving a higher score. The lack of exploring a novel model limits the potential significance and impact in the field.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Top-K Maximum Intensity Projection Priors for 3D Liver Vessel Segmentation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03367v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaotong Zhang, Alexander Broersen, Gonnie CM van Erp, Silvia L. Pintea, Jouke Dijkstra
- **Abstract**: Liver-vessel segmentation is an essential task in the pre-operative planning of liver resection. State-of-the-art 2D or 3D convolution-based methods focusing on liver vessel segmentation on 2D CT cross-sectional views, which do not take into account the global liver-vessel topology. To maintain this global vessel topology, we rely on the underlying physics used in the CT reconstruction process, and apply this to liver-vessel segmentation. Concretely, we introduce the concept of top-k maximum intensity projections, which mimics the CT reconstruction by replacing the integral along each projection direction, with keeping the top-k maxima along each projection direction. We use these top-k maximum projections to condition a diffusion model and generate 3D liver-vessel trees. We evaluate our 3D liver-vessel segmentation on the 3D-ircadb-01 dataset, and achieve the highest Dice coefficient, intersection-over-union (IoU), and Sensitivity scores compared to prior work.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach for 3D liver vessel segmentation using top-k maximum intensity projections (MIPs) as priors within a latent diffusion model (LDM).  The core idea is to mimic the CT reconstruction process, but instead of using integral projections, they keep the top-k maximum values along each projection direction to encode fine-grained 3D vessel structure information. These top-k MIPs are then used as conditions for an LDM to generate 3D liver vessel trees. The authors evaluate their method on the 3D-ircadb-01 dataset and claim superior Dice coefficient, IoU, and Sensitivity scores compared to existing methods. They also address artifact suppression through a post-processing optimization step to ensure projection consistency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The core concept of using top-k MIPs as priors for a diffusion model in vessel segmentation *is* a genuinely new contribution. Existing methods using MIPs typically only use the maximum value. Keeping the top-k maxima allows encoding more detailed 3D structure information which addresses a significant limitation of traditional MIP-based methods.
*   **Methodological Soundness:** The authors provide a clear explanation of their methodology, including the top-k MIP computation, the LDM conditioning, and the artifact suppression strategy. The connection between the top-k MIP and the CT reconstruction process is well-motivated.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The method is evaluated on a standard dataset (3D-ircadb-01), allowing for direct comparison with prior work. The reported results showing improved Dice, IoU, and Sensitivity suggest that the approach is effective.
*   **Addressing a Known Problem:** Liver vessel segmentation is a challenging task with significant clinical relevance.  The paper targets a key weakness of many existing methods – the lack of global topology awareness – in a principled way.
* **Artifact Suppression:** The post processing step with projection consistency optimization addresses a common problem in reconstruction and adds significant value to the overall pipeline.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Comparative Analysis:** While the paper compares against several existing methods, it would be beneficial to include a comparison against other techniques specifically designed to enforce topological constraints.  It would also be advantageous to report inference time, which is crucial for the real-world clinical applicability of the proposed method.
*   **Overclaims Regarding clDice:** The argument that the lower centerline Dice (clDice) score is primarily due to annotation inconsistencies is plausible, but it requires more robust justification.  Perhaps a qualitative assessment of the centerline extraction, or a more rigorous analysis of the annotation process, could strengthen this argument.
*   **Generalizability:** The method relies on specific parameters such as the number of projection directions and *k* (in top-*k* MIP). The paper lacks discussion or ablation experiments regarding the sensitivity of the method to these parameters, which impacts its practical usability.
* **Model Complexity and Reproducibility:** While the paper details the method conceptually, finer implementation details are lacking. This may hinder others ability to fully understand and reproduce the results presented.
* **No Statistical Significance:** The improvement claimed in table 1 lacks statistical significance testing for the results. This is crucial to determine the true impact of the method.

**Significance:**

The paper has the potential to influence the field of medical image segmentation, particularly for applications where maintaining global structure is crucial. The top-k MIP concept is a potentially generalizable idea that could be applied to other segmentation tasks. The focus on mimicking the CT reconstruction process provides a principled way to incorporate domain knowledge into deep learning models. The artifact suppression component, which compensates for inconsistency is also significant and clinically important.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-motivated approach to a challenging problem. The results, while promising, require more rigorous statistical analysis and further investigation into parameter sensitivity. The lack of detailed implementation information hinders the ability to reproduce and build upon this work, limiting its short term impact. Thus, while the core idea is strong, several aspects warrant further investigation.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### RASD: Retrieval-Augmented Speculative Decoding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03434v1)
- **Authors**: Guofeng Quan, Wenfeng Feng, Chuzhan Hao, Guochao Jiang, Yuewei Zhang, Hao Wang
- **Abstract**: Speculative decoding accelerates inference in large language models (LLMs) by generating draft tokens for target model verification. Current approaches for obtaining draft tokens rely on lightweight draft models or additional model structures to generate draft tokens and retrieve context from databases. Due to the draft model's small size and limited training data, model-based speculative decoding frequently becomes less effective in out-of-domain scenarios. Additionally, the time cost of the drafting phase results in a low upper limit on acceptance length during the verification step, limiting overall efficiency. This paper proposes RASD (Retrieval-Augmented Speculative Decoding), which adopts retrieval methods to enhance model-based speculative decoding. We introduce tree pruning and tree fusion to achieve this. Specifically, we develop a pruning method based on the draft model's probability distribution to construct the optimal retrieval tree. Second, we employ the longest prefix matching algorithm to merge the tree generated by the draft model with the retrieval tree, resulting in a unified tree for verification. Experimental results demonstrate that RASD achieves state-of-the-art inference acceleration across tasks such as DocQA, Summary, Code, and In-Domain QA. Moreover, RASD exhibits strong scalability, seamlessly integrating with various speculative decoding approaches, including both generation-based and retrieval-based methods.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RASD (Retrieval-Augmented Speculative Decoding), a method aimed at enhancing the performance of speculative decoding in Large Language Models (LLMs). Speculative decoding accelerates LLM inference by generating draft tokens that are then verified by the target model. RASD addresses limitations of existing approaches, which often rely on smaller draft models or additional model structures that struggle in out-of-domain scenarios.  RASD leverages retrieval methods to improve the quality of draft tokens. The core ideas include: (1) using a draft model to guide the selection of suitable retrieval results (tree pruning based on probability distributions), and (2) combining draft tokens with retrieved tokens using a tree fusion algorithm. The fused tree is then verified.  The authors demonstrate that RASD achieves state-of-the-art inference acceleration across various tasks and integrates seamlessly with different speculative decoding techniques (generation-based and retrieval-based). The method is evaluated with two retrieval algorithms PLD and REST.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of integrating retrieval with speculative decoding is a logical and reasonably novel extension of existing work. The authors are not the first to combine retrieval and generation but the specific application within the speculative decoding framework, coupled with the tree pruning and fusion techniques, introduces some originality. The main novelty lies in the *combination* of existing techniques and their careful application to address a specific problem in speculative decoding. This differs from completely novel algorithmic concepts, such as the Transformer architecture.
*   **Significance:** Improving LLM inference speed is a critical and highly relevant area of research. RASD presents a practical approach to achieve this, especially in scenarios where the draft model struggles (out-of-domain).  The experiments demonstrate consistent speedups compared to strong baselines, indicating practical benefits. The finding that the method is compatible with existing techniques adds to its significance. However, the improvement while significant is not dramatically over state of the art methods. The approach benefits most where existing draft models struggle and this niche restricts the impact.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of current speculative decoding methods.
    *   **Well-Defined Approach:** The proposed RASD method is clearly described, with sufficient detail on the tree pruning and fusion techniques.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The authors conduct thorough experiments across diverse tasks (DocQA, Summary, Code, QA) and models (Qwen2.5, LLaMA3), with two different retrieval methods. Ablation studies analyze the impact of key components.
    *   **Strong Results:** The results consistently show that RASD improves inference speed compared to baselines.
    *   **Scalability:** The method's claimed scalability and compatibility with other methods are positive.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While RASD provides improvements, the speedups might be considered incremental compared to EAGLE2, the current state-of-the-art method. While the margin is significant, the impact is less impressive.
    *   **Complexity:**  Integrating retrieval adds complexity to the decoding process, potentially increasing engineering overhead.
    *   **Retrieval Dependency:** The method's effectiveness is dependent on the quality of the retrieval database. The results shown also depend on the task and the most appropriate retrieval method.
    *   **Overhead:** While the paper notes that RASD is faster than auto-regressive decoding, it glosses over the additional overhead of generating draft tokens. If the accuracy of the draft is low, the time spent creating draft tokens is wasted. It would have been interesting to see an experiment where the same tasks and methods are tested with auto-regressive decoding.
    *   **Limited ablation on the selection of 'k'**: The method requires hyperparameters for tree pruning to be selected. The paper identifies that performance differs by task, indicating that significant tuning may be required in practice.
    *   **Parallelisation not considered** The paper notes that the draft token generation and retrieval process are performed serially. This limitation hinders the effectiveness of the approach as both are relatively computationally intensive.
*   **Potential Influence:** RASD could influence future research in speculative decoding by highlighting the benefits of integrating external knowledge sources. The tree pruning and fusion techniques could be adopted by other researchers. The result that model-based speculative decoding can be improved through better selection of draft tokens is significant.

**Justification for Score:**

RASD presents a valuable contribution to the field of LLM inference acceleration by demonstrating how retrieval methods can be integrated into speculative decoding. The paper is well-written, the approach is clearly defined, and the experiments are comprehensive and demonstrate convincing results. However, the gains, while significant, are incremental to SOTA, the retrieval adds complexity, the approach depends on the quality of retrieval database, and the method depends on hyperparameters to be effective. The serially processed method requires significant computation and reduces the speedup. Weighing these considerations, and considering the incremental nature of the improvement on SOTA, I would assign a score of:
